Global stability of vacuum for the relativistic Vlasov-Maxwell-Boltzmann system
Chuqi Cao, Xingyu Li

Abstract
We consider the three-dimensional relativistic Vlasov–Maxwell–Boltzmann system, where the speed of light c c is an arbitrary constant no less than 1, and we establish global existence and nonlinear stability of the vacuum for small initial data, with bounds that are uniform in c c . The analysis is based on the vector field method combined with the Glassey–Strauss decomposition of the electromagnetic field, and does not require any compact support assumption on the initial data. A key ingredient of the proof is the derivation of a chain rule for the relativistic Boltzmann collision operator that is compatible with the commutation properties of the vector fields. These tools allow us to control the coupled kinetic and electromagnetic equations and to obtain global stability near vacuum.



stix@largesymbols"0E
stix@largesymbols"0F

Keywords:
relativistic Vlasov–Maxwell-Boltzmann, asymptotic, stability.

1
Introduction

The collective dynamics of collision-dominated plasmas—central to astrophysics, fusion research, and space physics—are described at the microscopic level by kinetic theory. In relativistic regimes, the appropriate model is the relativistic Vlasov–Maxwell–Boltzmann (RVMB) system, which couples three fundamental mechanisms: relativistic transport of charged particles, self-consistent electromagnetic fields governed by Maxwell’s equations with finite speed of light
c
, and nonlinear particle interactions described by the Boltzmann collision operator.

The RVMB system models the time evolution of dilute ionized charged particles (e.g. electrons and ions), undergoing binary collisions while interacting through internally generated Lorentz forces. More precisely, the RVMB system reads

\partial_{t}f+\hat{v}\cdot\nabla_{x}f+\left(E+\frac{1}{c}\hat{v}\times B\right)\cdot\nabla_{v}f=Q_{c}(f,f),\\

(1.1)

\partial_{t}E=c\nabla\times B-J,\quad\nabla\cdot E=J_{0}(f),\quad\partial_{t}B=-c\nabla\times E,\quad\nabla\cdot B=0,

(1.2)

with initial data

f(0,x,v)=f_{0}(x,v),\quad E(0,x)=E_{0}(x),\quad B(0,x)=B_{0}(x),

for
t\geq 0,x,v\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
and speed of light
c\geq 1
. Here
f
denotes the density distribution function of the particles, and
E,B
are electric and the magnetic field respectively. Moreover,

J_{0}(f)(t,x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}f(t,x,v)\textnormal{d}v,\quad J_{i}(f)(t,x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\hat{v}_{i}f(t,x,v)\textnormal{d}v,\quad i=1,2,3,

(1.3)

are the total charge density and the total current density, and
\hat{v}=\frac{cv}{v_{0}}
denotes the relativistic speed of particles, and
v_{0}=\sqrt{c^{2}+v^{2}}
. The relativistic Boltzmann collision operator
Q_{c}
writes

Q_{c}(h,f)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}B_{c}(v,u)\left(h(u^{\prime})f(v^{\prime})-h(u)f(v)\right)\textnormal{d}\omega\textnormal{d}u:=Q_{c}^{+}(h,f)-Q_{c}^{-}(h,f),

(1.4)

here

•

g=\sqrt{2(v_{0}u_{0}-v\cdot w-c^{2})}
denotes the
relative momentum
, and
s=2(v_{0}u_{0}-v\cdot w+c^{2})
denotes the square of energy in the
center-of-momentum
. From direct calculations, we have
s=g^{2}+4c^{2}
.

•

B_{c}(v,w)=v_{\phi}\sigma(g,\vartheta)
, where
\sigma(g,\vartheta)\vcentcolon=|g|^{\gamma-1}\sigma_{0}(\vartheta)
is the scattering kernel, where
0\leq\sigma_{0}(\vartheta)\lesssim 1
, and the scattering angle
\vartheta
is defined as

\cos\vartheta=\frac{-(v_{0}-u_{0})(v_{0}^{\prime}-u_{0}^{\prime})+(v-u)(v^{\prime}-u^{\prime})}{g^{2}},

(1.5)

and
v_{\phi}=\frac{cg\sqrt{s}}{4v_{0}u_{0}}
denotes the Möller velocity.

•

v^{\prime}
and
u^{\prime}

stand for the post-collisional momenta of particles which had the momenta
v
and
u
before the collision, respectively. Then momentum and the energy are conserved after each collision, which is

v+u=v^{\prime}+u^{\prime},\quad v_{0}+u_{0}=v^{\prime}_{0}+u^{\prime}_{0}.

In the center-of-momentum frame, under the scattering angle
\omega
, we have

\displaystyle v^{\prime}=\frac{v+u}{2}+\frac{g}{2}\left(\omega+(\zeta-1)(v+u)\frac{(v+u)\cdot\omega}{|v+u|^{2}}\right),\quad u^{\prime}=\frac{v+u}{2}-\frac{g}{2}\left(\omega+(\zeta-1)(v+u)\frac{(v+u)\cdot\omega}{|v+u|^{2}}\right),

where
\zeta=\frac{u_{0}+v_{0}}{\sqrt{s}}
.

The RVMB system presents a significant challenge due to the strong coupling between hyperbolic Maxwell equations and the relativistic Boltzmann operator. Our analysis utilizes the commutator vector field method to control electromagnetic propagation and its feedback on the distribution function.

To overcome the lack of natural commutation between momentum-space integrals and Lorentz boosts, we establish specific commutation relations consistent with relativistic symmetry. This allows the vector field framework to be applied at the kinetic level despite the operator’s nonlinearity. By combining the Glassey–Strauss decomposition with refined energy estimates, we obtain bounds uniform for
c\geq 1
. This framework demonstrates that the coupling between Maxwellian hyperbolicity and the collision operator can be closed near vacuum, providing a robust pathway for studying relativistic stability.

1.1
Main result

Our main result is stated as follows.

Theorem 1.1

(Global stability of the relativistic Vlasov-Maxwell-Boltzmann system)
.

Let
\gamma\in(-2,0]
. For any
c\geq 1
,
M>0
, there exists a constant
\epsilon_{0}>0
independent of
c
, such that for any
\epsilon_{1}\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
, if the initial data
(f_{0},E_{0},B_{0})
satisfy

\|(1+|x|)^{1000}(1+|v|)^{1000}\nabla^{20}_{x,v}f_{0}(x,v)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}\leq\epsilon_{1},\quad\|(1+|x|)^{1000}\nabla^{20}_{x}E_{0}(x)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}+\|(1+|x|)^{1000}\nabla^{20}_{x}B_{0}(x)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq M,

(1.6)

then the relativistic Vlasov-Maxwell-Boltzmann system (
1.1
)-(
1.2
) admits a global solution. Moreover, we have the following estimates in
t
for distribution function

\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\langle v\rangle^{5}\hat{Z}^{\beta}f(t,x,v)\textnormal{d}v\right|\lesssim\epsilon_{1}(1+t+|x|)^{-3}\quad\textnormal{for all}\quad|\beta|\leq 18,

(1.7)

where
\hat{Z}
is defined in
Definition

2.1
, and the following optimal in
t
estimates for electromagnetic fields:

\left|E(t,x)\right|\lesssim\frac{M}{\left(1+t+\left|x\right|\right)\left(1+\left|t-\frac{\left|x\right|}{c}\right|\right)},\quad\left|B(t,x)\right|\lesssim\frac{M}{c\left(1+t+\left|x\right|\right)\left(1+\left|t-\frac{\left|x\right|}{c}\right|\right)}.

(1.8)

As a significant byproduct of our work, we establish the
first chain rule for the relativistic Boltzmann operator
. This result overcomes a long-standing structural barrier caused by the lack of symmetry in the relativistic collision kernel. We believe this identity is of independent mathematical interest and will serve as a fundamental tool for future research requiring high-order regularity in relativistic kinetic theory.

Theorem 1.2

(Chain rule for the relativistic Boltzmann operator)
.

For any smooth functions
h,f
we have

\partial_{v_{j}}Q_{c}(h,f)=\frac{1}{v_{0}}Q_{c}(h,(v_{0}\partial_{v_{j}}f))+\frac{1}{v_{0}}Q_{c}((v_{0}\partial_{v_{j}}h),f)-\frac{v_{j}}{v_{0}^{2}}Q_{c}(h,f),\quad v_{0}=\sqrt{c^{2}+v^{2}}.

(1.9)

We give several remarks in order.

Remark 1.3
.

All statements and results in Theorem
1.1
remain valid for the relativistic Vlasov-Maxwell system in the absence of the relativistic Boltzmann operator (i.e., the collisionless case).

Remark 1.4
.

In Theorem
1.1
we only require the initial distribution function
f_{0}
is small, and does not require any smallness assumption on the Maxwell field.

Remark 1.5
.

The estimates established in Theorem
1.1
are uniform for all speeds of light
c\geq 1
. This suggests the potential for convergence to the classical Vlasov-Poisson-Boltzmann equation:

\partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f+E\cdot\nabla_{v}f=Q(f,f),

(1.10)

with

\nabla\cdot E=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}f(t,x,v)\,dv,\quad\nabla\times E=0,

as
c\to\infty
, where
Q(f,f)
denotes the classical Boltzmann operator. However, the rigorous derivation of the Newtonian limit involves a delicate analysis of the difference between the relativistic operator
Q_{c}
and the classical operator
Q
. Furthermore, it requires a proof of the stability of vacuum for the Vlasov-Poisson-Boltzmann system using the vector field method. We postpone the treatment of this limit to future work.

Remark 1.6
.

The optimal decay rates obtained here are consistent with the sharp rates for the relativistic Vlasov-Maxwell system (
c=1
) reported in
[
4
,
54
,
56
]
. Moreover, by formally taking the limit
c\to\infty
, our decay estimates yield:

\left|E(t,x)\right|\lesssim\frac{1}{\left(1+t+\left|x\right|\right)\left(1+\left|t-\frac{\left|x\right|}{c}\right|\right)}\quad\to\quad\frac{1}{(1+t)^{2}},\quad\left|B(t,x)\right|\lesssim\frac{1}{c\left(1+t+\left|x\right|\right)\left(1+\left|t-\frac{\left|x\right|}{c}\right|\right)}\quad\to\quad 0,

This matches the optimal decay rate
E\lesssim(1+t)^{-2}
for the Vlasov-Poisson system established in
[
3
,
47
,
55
]
.

Remark 1.7
.

By utilizing (
3.8
) in Lemma
3.6
, one can show that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}f(t,x,v)\,\textnormal{d}x\to F(v)\quad\text{as }t\to\infty,

for some limiting distribution
F(v)
. This indicates that the solution scatters to a linear trajectory. The investigation of modified scattering, which requires more refined estimates, will be addressed in a subsequent paper.

Remark 1.8
.

For the sake of clarity, this paper focuses on the single-species relativistic Vlasov-Maxwell-Boltzmann equation. However, our methodology can be extended to the two-species system describing ions (
f_{+}
) and electrons (
f_{-}
):

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f_{+}+\hat{v}\cdot\nabla_{x}f_{+}+\big(E+\frac{1}{c}\hat{v}\times B\big)\cdot\nabla_{p}f_{+}=Q_{c}(f_{-},f_{+})+\left(f_{+},f_{+}\right),

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f_{-}+\hat{v}\cdot\nabla_{x}f_{-}-\big(E+\frac{1}{c}\hat{v}\times B\big)\cdot\nabla_{p}f_{-}=Q_{c}(f_{-},f_{-})+\left(f_{+},f_{-}\right),

\displaystyle\partial_{t}E-c\nabla_{x}\times B=-4\pi\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\Big(\hat{v}f_{+}-\hat{v}f_{-}\Big)\textnormal{d}v,\quad\partial_{t}B+c\nabla_{x}\times E=0,

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}\cdot E=4\pi\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left(f_{+}-f_{-}\right)\textnormal{d}v\quad\nabla_{x}\cdot B=0.

1.2
Related Works

In this subsection we will briefly review some former works related to us.

1.2.1
The relativistic Vlasov-Maxwell system

The mathematical analysis of kinetic equations has seen significant progress in both non-relativistic and relativistic regimes. A definitive challenge in this field remains the stability of the vacuum for the three-dimensional non-relativistic Vlasov-Maxwell system. In contrast, the relativistic setting was famously addressed by Glassey and Strauss
[
19
,
18
]
, who established the first global stability results for the relativistic Vlasov-Maxwell (RVM) system (see also
[
35
,
46
]
). Their methodology relied on the method of characteristics and necessitated the assumption of compactly supported initial data:

f_{0}(x,v)=0\quad\text{for}\quad|x|\geq M.

This assumption ensures that the solution retains finite spatial propagation,
f(t,x,v)=0
for
|x|\geq\beta(t)
, a property central to their characteristic-based arguments. However, a fundamental obstacle arises when extending this framework to the RVMB system: the non-locality of the Boltzmann collision operator in the momentum variable
v
immediately destroys the finite speed of propagation. The transport effect subsequently spreads this non-locality to the spatial variables, rendering the classical Glassey-Strauss approach inapplicable.

To address these limitations, recent research has focused on establishing vacuum stability for the RVM system without the compact support requirement. This has been achieved through several sophisticated analytical frameworks:

•

Wang
[
54
]
utilized modified vector fields, integrated with Fourier analytic techniques.

•

Bigorgne
[
4
]
used modified vector fields and Lie algebra-based analysis within Minkowski space.

•

Wei–Yang
[
56
]
developed an alternative approach to the method of characteristics.

•

Bigorgne
[
5
]
recently streamlined the theory by demonstrating that modified vector fields are not strictly necessary, providing a more direct proof.

While our current work draws inspiration from
[
5
]
, there are distinct differences in our approach. Whereas
[
5
]
operates in Minkowski space
(\mathbb{R}^{1+3},\eta)
with coordinates
(x_{0}=t,x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
and metric
\eta=(-1,1,1,1)
, we separate the time and space variables to explicitly track the dependence on the speed of light
c
. We provide an independent proof and anticipate that our framework for the Vlasov-Maxwell component remains parallel to
[
5
]
in the specific case where
c=1
.

Additionally, global well-posedness for the RVM system has been established under various symmetry assumptions
[
15
,
16
,
42
,
53
]
. For a broader discussion on continuation criteria, we refer to
[
19
,
17
,
6
,
48
,
44
,
37
]
, as well as
[
41
,
40
]
for stability and instability criteria.

1.2.2
Boltzmann and Landau Equations

The global well-posedness of kinetic equations near Maxwellian equilibrium is a well-established area of research. In the non-relativistic regime, existence for the cutoff Boltzmann equation was first established by Ukai
[
52
]
(see also
[
28
,
50
]
), while the non-cutoff case was addressed more recently in
[
23
,
1
,
2
]
. The Landau equation has been similarly treated by Guo
[
26
]
, with further developments in
[
8
,
13
]
. Coupled systems, such as the Vlasov-Poisson/Maxwell-Boltzmann and Landau systems, have been extensively studied near Maxwellians in
[
27
,
29
,
12
,
30
]
; for recent work on the Hilbert expansion in these contexts, see
[
38
]
.

In the relativistic regime, the structural complexity of collision operators significantly complicates the analysis. Global well-posedness for the relativistic Boltzmann equation with angular cutoff near a relativistic Maxwellian (Jüttner distribution) was first proved by Glassey and Strauss
[
20
,
21
]
. The angular non-cutoff case was subsequently resolved in
[
33
]
. Regarding coupled relativistic systems, Guo and Strain
[
24
]
established the stability of the Maxwellian for the RVMB system. We also refer to
[
39
,
49
,
57
]
for analogous results concerning the relativistic Landau and Vlasov-Maxwell-Landau equations.

Stability Near Vacuum

While the literature on Maxwellian stability is extensive, the problem of global stability near vacuum presents distinct challenges due to the lack of a dissipative spectral gap. In the non-relativistic setting, vacuum stability for cutoff Boltzmann equation was first proved in
[
31
]
, similar result for the Landau equation was addressed by Luk
[
43
]
, and the non-cutoff Boltzmann equation by Chaturvedi
[
10
]
. For the Vlasov-Poisson-Boltzmann system with angular cutoff, stability was established by Guo
[
25
]
.

To the best of our knowledge, no global stability results near vacuum currently exist for the Vlasov-Maxwell-Boltzmann system, in either the relativistic or non-relativistic framework. This work aims to fill that gap by providing a comprehensive stability analysis for the RVMB system.

1.3
Key ideas of proof

In this subsection, we illustrate the main ideas of the proof.

1.3.1
Null structure of electricmagnetic field

The electromagnetic field, represented by
E(t,x)
and
B(t,x)
, consists of six components. Among these, four specific linear combinations denoted by
\beta_{i}(E,B)
, exhibit enhanced decay rates. To characterize these, we introduce an orthonormal basis
\{e_{1}^{\prime},e_{2}^{\prime},e_{3}^{\prime}\}
associated with the spherical coordinates
x=r(\sin\theta\cos\phi,\sin\theta\sin\phi,\cos\theta)
:

e_{1}^{\prime}=(\sin\theta\cos\phi,\sin\theta\sin\phi,\cos\theta)=\frac{x}{r},\quad e_{2}^{\prime}=(\cos\theta\cos\phi,\cos\theta\sin\phi,-\sin\theta),\quad e_{3}^{\prime}=(-\sin\phi,\cos\phi,0).

We prove that the ’good’ components—namely the radial components and the null components, satisfy a faster decay rate of
(1+t)^{-(2-\varepsilon)}
for any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. These components are defined as:

\beta_{i}(E,B):=E\cdot e_{1}^{\prime},\quad B\cdot e_{1}^{\prime},\quad E\cdot e_{2}^{\prime}+B\cdot e_{3}^{\prime},\quad E\cdot e_{3}^{\prime}-B\cdot e_{2}^{\prime},\quad i=1,2,3,4.

The central strategy relies on bounding the Lorentz force term by the sum of these decaying components and a geometric weight:

\left|E+\frac{v}{v_{0}}\times B\right|\lesssim\left(1-\frac{v}{v_{0}}\cdot\frac{x}{|x|}\right)(|E|+|B|)+\sum_{i=1}^{4}|\beta_{i}(E,B)|.

A detailed derivation of these estimates is provided in
Section

2.4
.

1.3.2
Vector field method and
c
-dependent vector field

The vector field method, pioneered by Klainerman
[
36
]
and later extended to the Maxwell equations by Christodoulou and Klainerman
[
11
]
, has become a cornerstone in the global analysis of nonlinear partial differential equations. In the context of kinetic theory, Smulevici
[
47
]
first adapted these techniques to the Vlasov-Poisson system. This was subsequently followed by the work of Fajman, Joudioux, and Smulevici
[
14
]
, who established a robust framework for relativistic transport equations using commutation properties of the relativistic velocity vector fields.

More recently, these methods have been integrated into the study of kinetic models featuring collision terms, most notably the Boltzmann and Landau equations. Unlike the analysis of dissipation near a global Maxwellian, establishing global stability near vacuum necessitates sharp, time-decaying estimates of the full collision operators. Building upon Luk’s
[
43
]
application of vector fields to the Landau equation, Chaturvedi
[
10
]
established analogous stability results for the Boltzmann equation in the non-cutoff regime. This trajectory of research was further advanced by Chaturvedi, Luk, and Nguyen
[
9
]
, who treated the Vlasov-Poisson-Landau system in the regime of weak collisions.

1: The
c
-Dependent Formulation.

In this paper, we introduce and utilize a family of
c
-dependent vector fields designed to track the reliance on the speed of light
c\geq 1
. For a function of
(t,x)
and
c\geq 1
, we denote
Z
as the
c
-dependent vector field combined with the following operators

\frac{1}{c}\partial_{t},\quad\partial_{x_{i}},\quad S=\frac{1}{c}\left(t\partial_{t}+\sum_{i}x_{i}\partial_{x_{i}}\right),\quad\Omega_{i}=t\partial_{x_{i}}+\frac{1}{c^{2}}x_{i}\partial_{t},\quad\Omega_{jk}=x_{j}\partial_{x_{k}}-x_{k}\partial_{x_{j}},\quad i,j,k=1,2,3,\quad j\neq k,

and we denote
\hat{Z}
as the complete lift of
Z
, which is the
c
-dependent vector field combined with the following operators

\frac{1}{c}\partial_{t},\,\,\partial_{x_{i}},\,\,S=\frac{1}{c}\left(t\partial_{t}+\sum_{i}x_{i}\partial_{x_{i}}\right),\,\,\hat{\Omega}_{i}=t\partial_{x_{i}}+\frac{1}{c^{2}}x_{i}\partial_{t}+\frac{v_{0}}{c}\partial_{v_{i}},\,\,\hat{\Omega}_{jk}=x_{j}\partial_{x_{k}}-x_{k}\partial_{x_{j}}+v_{j}\partial_{v_{k}}-v_{k}\partial_{v_{j}},\,\,i,j,k=1,2,3,\,\,j\neq k.

Z
and
\hat{Z}
are used in
[
47
,
14
]
for
c=1
. In this paper,
\hat{Z}
is applied to
f(t,x,v)
, and
Z
is applied to
E(t,x),B(t,x)
. In particular, for any function
G(t,x)
that only depends on
t,x
we have
\hat{Z}(G(t,x))=Z(G(t,x))
for any function
G(t,x)
. In the Newtonian limit

c\to\infty
,
\hat{\Omega}_{i}
converges to the non-relativistic lift
t\partial_{x_{i}}+\partial_{v_{i}}
, consistent with the vector fields used in non-relativistic studies
[
43
,
10
]
. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first usage of a
c
-dependent commutator framework that is uniform for all
c\geq 1
, effectively bridging the relativistic and non-relativistic regimes.

2: Advantages for Decay and Commutation.

A primary advantage of this framework is the retrieval of
enhanced decay rates
. By expressing derivatives in terms of the scaling and boost operators, we observe:

\frac{1}{c}\partial_{t}=\frac{t}{(t+\frac{|x|}{c})(t-\frac{|x|}{c})}S-\sum_{i}\frac{x_{i}}{c(t+\frac{|x|}{c})(t-\frac{|x|}{c})}\Omega_{i},

and

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{i}}=

\displaystyle\frac{t}{(t+\frac{|x|}{c})(t-\frac{|x|}{c})}\Omega_{0,i}-\frac{x_{i}}{c(t+\frac{|x|}{c})(t-\frac{|x|}{c})}S-\sum_{j}\frac{x_{j}}{c^{2}(t+\frac{|x|}{c})(t-\frac{|x|}{c})}\Omega_{ij}.

Thus an extra
\frac{1}{|t-\frac{|x|}{c}|}
decay rate is obtained for
\partial_{x_{i}},\frac{1}{c}\partial_{t}
, together with (
1.13
) could imply an extra
(1+t)^{-1}
decay. Moreover, recall that
\hat{\Omega}_{0,i}=t\partial_{x_{i}}+\frac{1}{c^{2}}x_{i}\partial_{t}+\frac{v_{0}}{c}\partial_{v_{i}}
.
Then we have

\partial_{v_{i}}=\frac{c}{v_{0}}\left(\hat{\Omega}_{0,i}-t\partial_{x_{i}}-\frac{1}{c^{2}}x_{i}\partial_{t}\right).

Thus the term
(E+\frac{v}{v_{0}}\times B)\cdot\nabla_{v}f
can also be written in the form of
\hat{Z}
. Also the
c
-dependent vector field commutes well with the transport operator
T_{0}:=\partial_{t}+\hat{v}\cdot\nabla_{x}
and the Maxwell equation. For
T_{0}
we have that

[v_{0}T_{0},\hat{Z}]=0,\quad\hat{Z}=\frac{1}{c}\partial_{t},\partial_{x_{i}},\hat{\Omega}_{i},\hat{\Omega}_{i,j},\quad[v_{0}T_{0},S]=\frac{1}{c}v_{0}T_{0},

where
[A,B]=AB-BA
denotes the Poisson bracket of two operators. The framework also commutes efficiently with the wave equations satisfied by the electromagnetic fields
(E,B)
:

\partial_{t}^{2}E-c^{2}\Delta_{x}E=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(c^{2}\nabla_{x}+\hat{v}\partial_{t})f\textnormal{d}v,\quad\partial_{t}^{2}B-c^{2}\Delta_{x}B=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}c(\nabla_{x}\times\hat{v})f\textnormal{d}v.

More precisely, for
(f,E,B)
as the solution of the RVMB system(
1.1
), (
1.2
), denote

h_{1}^{i}(f):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(c^{2}\partial_{x_{i}}+\frac{cv_{i}}{v_{0}}\partial_{t})f\textnormal{d}v,\quad h_{2}^{i,j}(f):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left(\frac{c^{2}v_{i}}{v_{0}}\partial_{x_{j}}-\frac{c^{2}v_{j}}{v_{0}}\partial_{x_{i}}\right)f\textnormal{d}v,

then we have that

(\partial_{t}^{2}-c^{2}\Delta_{x})(Z^{\alpha}[E,B])=\sum_{\beta\leq\alpha}\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{3}C_{\alpha,\beta,i}h_{1}^{i}(\hat{Z}^{\beta}f)+C_{\alpha,\beta,j,k}h_{2}^{j,k}(\hat{Z}^{\beta}f),

where
C_{\alpha,\beta,i}
and
C_{\alpha,\beta,j,k}
are uniformly bounded constants with respect to
c
, ensuring that our analysis holds robustly as
c\to\infty
. Detailed statements and proofs of these commutation identities are provided in
Lemma

2.18
.

1.3.3
Weight Functions and the Distribution Function Estimate

A central component of our analysis is the choice of weight function and its role in controlling the distribution function
f
. We define

\langle\cdot\rangle=(1+\left|\cdot\right|^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}
. The weight function of interest is
\langle x-t\hat{v}\rangle
, which is particularly natural for transport-dominated equations because it commutes with the free transport operator
\partial_{t}+\hat{v}\cdot\nabla_{x}f
. Moreover, if we denote
h(t,x,v)=f(t,x+\hat{v}t,v)
, then we have

\langle x\rangle h(t,x,v)=\langle x-\hat{v}t\rangle f(t,x,v).

(1.11)

The utility of this weight is twofold. First, it allows us to recover sharp decay in the spatial density. As shown in Lemmas
2.5
, that the
L^{1}_{v}
norm of
f
satisfies:

\|f\|_{L^{1}_{v}}\lesssim(1+t)^{-3}\|\langle v\rangle^{5}\langle x-\hat{v}t\rangle^{4}\|_{L^{\infty}_{v}}.

(1.12)

Second, it provides a mechanism to relate the weights to the physical trajectories of the system. Specifically, from
Lemma

2.8
we have the following inequality:

\quad 1+t+|x|\lesssim\left(1+\left|t-\frac{|x|}{c}\right|\right)\langle v\rangle^{4}\langle x-\hat{v}t\rangle^{2}.

(1.13)

This inequality is crucial for transforming the electromagnetic field estimates in (
1.8
) into integrable terms of order
(1+t)^{-2}
, at the expense of additional weight factors. In our framework, we classify terms of the form
\langle v\rangle^{N_{1}}\langle x-\hat{v}t\rangle^{N_{2}}f
as higher-order terms. A primary objective of this work is to prove that these quantities—even when subjected to the commutation vector fields
\hat{Z}^{\alpha}
—grow no faster than logarithmically in time. For the precise formulation of these bounds, we refer the reader to Proposition
3.5
.

To establish the logarithmic bound on these higher-order terms, we recall that the distribution function
f
satisfies (
1.1
). Let
T_{F}
denotes the Vlasov operator

T_{F}=\partial_{t}+\hat{v}\cdot\nabla_{x}-\left(E+\frac{v}{v_{0}}\times B\right)\cdot\nabla_{v},

then we have
T_{F}(f)=Q_{c}(f,f)
. When commuting
T_{F}
with the vector fields
\hat{Z}^{\alpha}
, we obtain:

T_{F}(\hat{Z}^{\alpha}f)=\textnormal{Boltzmann term}+\left(E+\frac{v}{v_{0}}\times B\right)\cdot\nabla_{v}\hat{Z}^{\alpha}f+\textnormal{commutator terms of}\,\,f,E,B.

The Boltzmann terms are handled in
Section

5
, where we show they decay at a rate of
(1+t)^{-\lambda}
for
\lambda>1
, making them integrable in time. The primary difficulty lies in the commutator terms involving
f,E,
and
B
. A naive estimate suggests:

\textnormal{commutator terms of}\,\,f,E,B\sim t^{-1}|\partial_{t,x}f|.

The argument cannot be directly closed since
t^{-1}
is not integrable. This necessitates a refined analysis of the vector fields
\hat{Z}
.

We decompose the vector fields into "good terms" (e.g.,
\frac{1}{t}\partial_{t},\partial_{x}
) and "bad terms" (e.g., the rotations
\hat{\Omega}_{i},\hat{\Omega}_{jk}
). By leveraging the inquality (
1.13
) and the null structure of the electromagnetic field, we obtain the following improved estimates:

•

Good Commutators:
\lesssim\frac{1}{(1+t)\log^{2}(3+t)}
,

•

Bad Commutators:
\lesssim\frac{1-\frac{\hat{v}\cdot x}{c|x|}}{\left(1+\left|t-\frac{|x|}{c}\right|\right)\log^{2}\left(3+\left|t-\frac{|x|}{c}\right|\right)}.

By applying Lemma
2.28
, we prove that these commutator terms are integrable in time. This ensures that the higher-order terms grow at most logarithmically, which, combined with (
1.12
), allows us to conclude that
\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\hat{Z}^{\alpha}f\,dv\right|\lesssim t^{-3}
.

1.3.4
The relativistic Boltzmann collision operator

In this subsection, we address the technical challenges for the relativistic Boltzmann collision operator, specifically focusing on the lack of a standard chain rule, the construction of weight functions, and the selection of the collision index.

1: Chain rule for the relativistic Boltzmann operator.

A central challenge in this analysis stems from a fundamental structural difference between the relativistic and classical theories. In the classical setting, it is well known that the Boltzmann operator
Q(f,f)
satisfies a Leibniz-type rule with respect to velocity derivatives:

\partial_{v_{i}}Q(f,f)=Q(\partial_{v_{i}}f,f)+Q(f,\partial_{v_{i}}f).

This property is a direct consequence of the collision kernel’s structure. For classical kernels of the form
|v-v_{*}|^{n}
, the following translational symmetry holds:
\partial_{v_{i}}(|v-v_{*}|^{n})=-\partial_{v_{i,*}}(|v-v_{*}|^{n})
. This symmetry is essential for distributing velocity derivatives across the arguments of the bilinear operator via integration by parts.

However, this property does not generalize to the relativistic collision kernel. The lack of such symmetry prevents the derivative from commuting with the operator in a straightforward manner. This failure of the standard chain rule has historically restricted the study of the relativistic Boltzmann equation, with most existing literature focusing on the
H^{k}_{x}L^{2}_{v}
or
L^{2}\cap L^{\infty}
frameworks.

In this paper, by utilizing the relativistic Carleman representation (Lemma
5.5
) established in
[
33
]
, we overcome this obstruction. We prove that the relativistic Boltzmann operator satisfies the following modified chain rule:

v_{0}\partial_{v_{j}}Q_{c}(h,f)=Q_{c}(h,v_{0}\partial_{v_{j}}f)+Q_{c}(v_{0}\partial_{v_{j}}h,f)-\frac{v_{j}}{v_{0}}Q_{c}(h,f),

as well as

(v_{j}\partial_{v_{i}}-v_{i}\partial_{v_{j}})Q_{c}(h,f)=Q_{c}(h,(v_{j}\partial_{v_{i}}-v_{i}\partial_{v_{j}})f)+Q_{c}((v_{j}\partial_{v_{i}}-v_{i}\partial_{v_{j}})h,f),

for any smooth functions
f
and
h
. We provide the complete proofs for these identities in
Section

5.3
.

The two chain rules imply that the relativistic Boltzmann operator is compatible with the vector field method, which strengthen the validity of our proof. Specifically, the following commutation identities hold:

\hat{Z}Q_{c}(f,g)=Q_{c}(\hat{Z}f,g)+Q_{c}(f,\hat{Z}g),\quad\hat{Z}\in\{\frac{1}{c}\partial_{t},\partial_{x_{i}},\hat{\Omega}_{ij},S\}.

Furthermore, for
\hat{\Omega}_{i}
, we have:

\hat{\Omega}_{i}Q_{c}(f,g)=Q_{c}(\hat{\Omega}_{i}f,g)+Q_{c}(f,\hat{\Omega}_{i}g)-\frac{v_{i}}{v_{0}}Q_{c}(f,g).

Since the factor
\frac{v_{i}}{v_{0}}
and its velocity derivatives are uniformly bounded from above , these identities facilitate the estimation of higher-order derivatives.

2: Estimate of the weight function in relativistic regime.

To control the Boltzmann operator within our weighted framework, we require a robust upper bound for the term
\langle x-t\hat{v}\rangle Q_{c}(h,f)
. This estimate involves a significant technical hurdle. We begin by decomposing the collision operator into its gain and loss components,
Q_{c}^{+}
and
Q_{c}^{-}
, respectively.

The loss term,
Q_{c}^{-}
, follows a structure of the form
h(u)f(v)
, which allows us to straightforwardly bound
\langle x-t\hat{v}\rangle Q_{c}^{-}(h,f)
using the product of
|h(u)|
and
\langle x-t\hat{v}\rangle|f(v)|
. In contrast, the gain term
Q_{c}^{+}
—defined by the post-collisional velocities
f(v^{\prime})h(u^{\prime})
—presents a greater difficulty. Ideally, one would utilize a sub-additive property for the weight, such as:

\langle x-t\hat{v}\rangle\lesssim\langle x-t\hat{v^{\prime}}\rangle+\langle x-t\hat{u^{\prime}}\rangle.

(1.14)

In the relativistic regime, however, (
1.14
) does not generally hold. Instead, we establish a weaker variant (see Lemma
5.3
) that incorporates a polynomial loss in the momentum variables:

\langle x-t\hat{v}\rangle\lesssim\min\{\langle v^{\prime}\rangle^{2},\langle u^{\prime}\rangle^{2}\}(\langle x-t\hat{v^{\prime}}\rangle+\langle x-t\hat{u^{\prime}}\rangle).

(1.15)

Because (
1.15
) is weaker than the standard estimate, it cannot be used directly to close the energy arguments. To circumvent this, we leverage the property
\langle v\rangle\lesssim\langle v^{\prime}\rangle+\langle u^{\prime}\rangle
and define a composite weight function:

\mathpzc n(v)\vcentcolon=\langle x-t\hat{v}\rangle^{k}\langle v\rangle^{4k+50}+\langle x-t\hat{v}\rangle^{k+10}\langle v\rangle^{2k+20},

for a sufficiently large
k\in\mathbb{N}
. We demonstrate that this refined weight satisfies the desired bound
\mathpzc n(v)\lesssim\mathpzc n(v^{\prime})+\mathpzc n(u^{\prime})
, thereby allowing us to close the argument by estimating
\left|\mathpzc n(v)Q_{c}(h,f)\right|
for smooth functions
f
and
h
. Detailed derivations are provided in Section
5.12
.

3: Rationale for the Collision Index
\gamma\in(-2,0]
.

We justify the range
\gamma\in(-2,0]
necessary for the global-in-time theory of the relativistic Boltzmann equation.

•

Upper bound (
\gamma\leq 0
): This constraint ensures control over the collision integral. For
\gamma>0
, the kernel
|u-v|^{\gamma}
grows unbounded as
|u-v|\to\infty
, preventing the integral from being bounded by standard
L^{p}_{v}
norms. While techniques exist in isolated Boltzmann theory to manage this growth, they produce extra unbounded terms when coupled with the relativistic Vlasov-Maxwell system. Thus,
\gamma\leq 0
is required to tame the collision term’s growth.

It is important to note, however, that this restriction can be relaxed under specific scaling. If we set the speed of light
c=1
, the constraint may be relaxed to
\gamma\leq 1
. This is possible because the term
v_{\phi}
includes the factor
\frac{c}{v_{0}}
, which behaves asymptotically like
\langle v\rangle^{-1}
when
c=1
, providing the necessary decay to offset the kernel’s growth.

•

Lower bound (
\gamma>-2
): This restriction is dictated by the time-integrability of the nonlinear decay. As shown in
Theorem

5.12
, the weighted nonlinear term satisfies:

|Q_{c}(h,f)|\lesssim(1+t)^{-{3+\gamma}}.

Global existence requires this rate to be integrable, implying
3+\gamma>1
, or equivalently,
\gamma>-2
. This restriction coincides with the restriction for the global stability of Landau and Vlasov-Poisson-Boltzmann equation near vacuum, see
[
25
,
43
]
.

Together, these conditions restrict our analysis to moderately soft potentials, ensuring the nonlinear contributions remain finite for all
t\in[0,\infty)
.

1.4
Structure of the paper

In
Section

2
, we introduce preliminary estimates and the algebraic properties of the commutator vector fields. In
Section

3
, we define bootstrap assumptions and improve the estimate of distribution function
f
. In
Section

4
, we improve the estimate of electromagnetic field functions
E,B
from Glassey-Strauss representation formula for the relativistic Vlasov-Maxwell system.
Section

5
establishes all the estimates for the relativistic Boltzmann operator and proves the associated chain rule, for readers only interested in the relativistic Boltzmann part, we refer to Section
5
.

The authors would thank Prof. Xuecheng Wang for introducing this problem and Prof. Renjun Duan for fruitful talks on the relativistic Boltzmann operator. CC would thank to The Chinese University of Hong Kong and The Hong Kong Polytechnic University for the support. XL is supported by PRIN 2022 ”Turbulent effects vs Stability in Equations from Oceanography” (TESEO), project number: 2022HSSYPN.

2
Preliminaries

We establish the foundational estimates required for our analysis and derive the algebraic properties of the commutator vector fields.

2.1
Notations and definitions

Let us first list some notations and definitions that will be used in the following sections of the paper.

Definition 2.1
.

We denote that

\langle\cdot\rangle=(1+\left|\cdot\right|^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}},r:=|x|,\kappa(v)=1-\frac{v\cdot x}{v_{0}|x|}=1-\frac{\hat{v}\cdot x}{cr}
.
Denote the transport operators

T_{0}=\partial_{t}+\hat{v}\cdot\nabla_{x},\quad T_{F}=\partial_{t}+\hat{v}\cdot\nabla_{x}-\left(E+\frac{v}{v_{0}}\times B\right)\cdot\nabla_{v}.

we define, on the domain
\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{3}||y|<c\}
, the operator
\check{\;\;}
as

y\to\check{y}=\frac{y}{\sqrt{1-\frac{|y|^{2}}{c^{2}}}},\quad\forall|y|<c,\quad\hat{\check{y}}=y,\quad\check{\hat{v}}=v,\quad\forall\quad v\in\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}.

(2.1)

For any
N_{1},N_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
, we define the
weight function

\mathpzc W^{N_{1}}_{N_{2}}(t,x,v,c)\vcentcolon=\langle v\rangle^{N_{1}}\langle x-t\hat{v}\rangle^{N_{2}},

(2.2)

and we will write
\mathpzc W^{N_{1}}_{N_{2}}
for short.

We denote

e_{1}=(1,0,0),\quad e_{2}=(0,1,0),\quad e_{3}=(0,0,1)
.
For
x=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
, under the sphere coordinates
(r,\theta,\phi)
, we have

x_{1}=r\sin\theta\cos\phi,\quad x_{2}=r\sin\theta\sin\phi,\quad x_{3}=r\cos\theta.

(2.3)

Using spherical coordinates for
x
we have that
x=r(\sin\theta\cos\phi,\sin\theta\sin\phi,\cos\theta)
, and we will denote that

e_{1}^{\prime}:=(\sin\theta\cos\phi,\sin\theta\sin\phi,\cos\theta)=\frac{x}{r},\quad e_{2}^{\prime}:=(\cos\theta\cos\phi,\cos\theta\sin\phi,-\sin\theta),\quad e_{3}^{\prime}:=(-\sin\phi,\cos\phi,0).

(2.4)

We can see that
e_{1}^{\prime},e_{2}^{\prime},e_{3}^{\prime}
also forms a new orthogonal base.
From direct calculations,

\partial_{r}=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\frac{x_{i}}{r}\partial_{x_{i}}=\frac{1}{r}x\cdot\nabla=e_{1}^{\prime}\cdot\nabla.

(2.5)

For
i,j,k=1,2,3,j\neq k
, let
\mathbb{K}
be the set composed by the vector fields

\frac{1}{c}\partial_{t},\quad\partial_{x_{i}},\quad\Omega_{jk}=x_{j}\partial_{x_{k}}-x_{k}\partial_{x_{j}},\quad\Omega_{i}=t\partial_{x_{i}}+\frac{1}{c^{2}}x_{i}\partial_{t},\quad S=\frac{1}{c}\left(t\partial_{t}+\sum_{i}x_{i}\partial_{x_{i}}\right),

and let
\tilde{\mathbb{K}}
be the set composed by the vector fields

\frac{1}{c}\partial_{t},\quad\partial_{x_{i}},\quad\hat{\Omega}_{jk}=x_{j}\partial_{x_{k}}-x_{k}\partial_{x_{j}}+v_{j}\partial_{v_{k}}-v_{k}\partial_{v_{j}},\quad\hat{\Omega}_{i}=t\partial_{x_{i}}+\frac{1}{c^{2}}x_{i}\partial_{t}+\frac{v_{0}}{c}\partial_{v_{i}},\quad S=\frac{1}{c}\left(t\partial_{t}+\sum_{i}x_{i}\partial_{x_{i}}\right).

These vector fields have good commutation properties with the linear transport operator
T_{0}=\partial_{t}+\hat{v}\cdot\nabla_{x}
(see
Lemma

2.25
for more details). In order to consider higher order derivatives, we introduce an ordering on
\mathbb{K}=\{Z^{i},1\leq i\leq 11\}
and
\tilde{\mathbb{K}}=\{\hat{Z}^{i},1\leq i\leq 11\}
.For a multi-index
\beta
, we denote
H(\beta)(T(\beta))
as the number of homogeneous vector fields, i.e.
S,\hat{\Omega}_{i},\hat{\Omega}_{ij}
(respectively translations, i.e.
\frac{1}{c}\partial_{t},\partial_{x_{i}}
) composing
\hat{Z}^{\beta}
. Sometimes we will denote
H(\hat{Z}^{\alpha}):=H(\alpha)

For any vector functions
F(t,x),G(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, denote
null decomposition

\rho([F,G])=F\cdot e_{1}^{\prime}\quad\sigma([F,G])=G\cdot e_{1}^{\prime},\quad\alpha_{1}([F,G])=F\cdot e_{2}^{\prime}+G\cdot e_{3}^{\prime},\quad\alpha_{2}([F,G])=F\cdot e_{2}^{\prime}-G\cdot e_{3}^{\prime},

and
\mathpzc D_{Z}(F,G)(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{6}
as

\mathpzc D_{Z}(F,G)=(\mathpzc D^{(1)}_{Z}(F,G),\mathpzc D^{(2)}_{Z}(F,G)):=\begin{cases}(ZF,ZG),\quad Z=\frac{1}{c}\partial_{t},\partial_{x_{i}},S\\
\left(ZF+\frac{1}{c}(0,-G_{3},G_{2}),ZG+\frac{1}{c}(0,F_{3},-F_{2})\right),\quad Z=\Omega_{1}\\
\left(ZF+(F_{2},-F_{1},0),ZG+(G_{2},-G_{1},0)\right),\quad Z=\Omega_{12},\end{cases}

and
Z=\Omega_{2},\Omega_{3},\Omega_{13},\Omega_{23}
can be defined similarly.

Remark 2.2
.

In Minkowski spacetime
(\mathbb{R}^{1+3},\eta)
, the vector fields in
\mathbb{K},\tilde{\mathbb{K}}
are categorized as follows:

•

Lorentz Boosts (
\Omega_{i}
) and Rotations (
\Omega_{jk}
): These, along with translations
\frac{1}{t}\partial_{t}
and
\partial_{x_{i}}
, form the set
\mathbb{K}\setminus\{S\}
and are Killing vector fields. They generate isometries that preserve the spacetime metric
\eta
.

•

Scaling (
S
): Also called dilatation, this is a conformal Killing vector field. It preserves the light-cone structure but alters the metric scale.

•

Complete Lifts (
\hat{\Omega}_{i},\hat{\Omega}_{jk}
): These represent the extension of the Lorentz generators to the tangent bundle, allowing the symmetries to act on both positions and velocities.

Remark 2.3
.

Remind that
F(t,x)
do not depend on
v
, so
Z^{i}F=\hat{Z}^{i}F(1\leq i\leq 11)
.

2.2
Vector Identities and Functional Inequalities

In this section, we establish the algebraic foundation and analytical estimates required for the subsequent proofs. We begin by recalling several fundamental vector identities.

Lemma 2.4
.

For any 3D vectors
{\textbf{a}},{\textbf{b}},{\textbf{c}},{\textbf{d}}
, we have

\displaystyle({\textbf{a}}\times{\textbf{b}})\cdot({\textbf{c}}\times{\textbf{d}})=({\textbf{a}}\cdot{\textbf{c}})({\textbf{b}}\cdot{\textbf{d}})-({\textbf{a}}\cdot{\textbf{d}})({\textbf{b}}\cdot{\textbf{c}}),\quad({\textbf{a}}\times{\textbf{b}})\times{\textbf{c}}={\textbf{b}}({\textbf{a}}\cdot{\textbf{c}})-{\textbf{a}}({\textbf{b}}\cdot{\textbf{c}}),\quad{\textbf{a}}\cdot({\textbf{b}}\times{\textbf{c}})={\textbf{b}}\cdot({\textbf{c}}\times{\textbf{a}})={\textbf{c}}\cdot({\textbf{a}}\times{\textbf{b}}),

(2.6a)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\qquad\quad\quad\quad\qquad\quad\quad\quad\qquad({\textbf{a}}\times{\textbf{b}})\times{\textbf{c}}+({\textbf{b}}\times{\textbf{c}})\times{\textbf{a}}+({\textbf{c}}\times{\textbf{a}})\times{\textbf{b}}=0,

(2.6b)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\qquad\quad\quad\quad\qquad\quad\quad\quad\qquad{\textbf{a}}{\textbf{b}}+{\textbf{c}}{\textbf{d}}=\frac{1}{2}[({\textbf{a}}+{\textbf{c}})({\textbf{b}}+{\textbf{d}})+({\textbf{a}}-{\textbf{c}})({\textbf{b}}-{\textbf{d}})],

(2.6c)

\displaystyle{\textbf{a}}\times e_{2}^{\prime}=({\textbf{a}}\cdot e_{1}^{\prime})e_{3}^{\prime}-({\textbf{a}}\cdot e_{3}^{\prime})e_{1}^{\prime},\quad{\textbf{a}}\times e_{3}^{\prime}=({\textbf{a}}\cdot e_{2}^{\prime})e_{1}^{\prime}-({\textbf{a}}\cdot e_{1}^{\prime})e_{2}^{\prime},\quad{\textbf{a}}\times e_{1}^{\prime}=({\textbf{a}}\cdot e_{3}^{\prime})e_{2}^{\prime}-({\textbf{a}}\cdot e_{2}^{\prime})e_{3}.

(2.6d)

The following lemmas provide the necessary
L^{p}
estimates and integral bounds used in our main proofs.

Lemma 2.5
.

For any smooth function
f
, for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{3},t\geq 0,c\geq 1,p\in[1,\infty)
, we have that

\displaystyle\|f\|_{L^{p}_{v}}\lesssim(1+t)^{-\frac{3}{p}}\|\langle v\rangle^{5}\langle x-t\hat{v}\rangle^{4}f\|_{L^{\infty}_{v}},

where the constant is independent of
x,t,c
.

Proof.

For
t\leq 1
this is just follows by

\|f\|_{L^{1}_{v}}\lesssim\|\langle v\rangle^{4}f\|_{L^{\infty}_{v}}
. For
t\geq 1
, from direct calculations,

\frac{\partial\hat{v}_{i}}{\partial v_{j}}=\frac{c\delta_{ij}}{v_{0}}-\frac{cv_{i}v_{j}}{v_{0}^{3}}=\frac{c}{v_{0}}\left[\delta_{ij}-\frac{v_{i}v_{j}}{v_{0}^{2}}\right],

which implies that

\det\left|\frac{\textnormal{d}\hat{v}}{\textnormal{d}v}\right|=\frac{c^{3}}{v_{0}^{3}}\det\left|I_{3\times 3}-\frac{v}{v_{0}}\otimes\frac{v}{v_{0}}\right|=\frac{c^{3}}{v_{0}^{3}}\left(1-|\frac{v}{v_{0}}|^{2}\right)=\frac{c^{5}}{v_{0}^{5}}.

Thus for any
(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
, the Jacobian determinant of the transformation
v\mapsto x-\widehat{v}t
is equal to
-\frac{c^{5}t^{3}}{|v^{0}|^{5}}
.
Thus for any smooth function
h
, remind the operator
y\to\check{y}
defined in (
2.1
), we have that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}t^{3}h(t,x-t\hat{v},v)\textnormal{d}v=\int_{|y-x|<ct}\frac{v_{0}^{5}}{c^{5}}h\left(t,y,\widecheck{\frac{x-y}{t}}\right)\textnormal{d}y.

(2.7)

Next for any
k>3
we have that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\langle x-t\hat{v}\rangle^{-k}t^{3}\langle v\rangle^{-5}\textnormal{d}v\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\langle x-t\hat{v}\rangle^{-k}t^{3}\frac{c^{5}}{v_{0}^{5}}\textnormal{d}v\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\langle u\rangle^{-k}\textnormal{d}u\lesssim 1,\quad\forall t\geq 0,\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{3},

thus for any
p\in[1,\infty)
, we have

\displaystyle\|f\|_{L^{p}_{v}}=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}|f|^{p}(v)\textnormal{d}v\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\lesssim\|\langle v\rangle^{5}\langle x-t\hat{v}\rangle^{4}f\|_{L^{\infty}_{v}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\langle x-t\hat{v}\rangle^{-4p}\langle v\rangle^{-5p}\textnormal{d}v\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\lesssim t^{-\frac{3}{p}}\|\langle v\rangle^{5}\langle x-t\hat{v}\rangle^{4}f\|_{L^{\infty}_{v}}.

The proof is thus finished.
∎

Lemma 2.6
.

For any smooth function
f
,
\gamma\in(-3,0)
fixed, and any
p>1
that satisfies
p>\frac{3}{3+\gamma}
i.e.
\frac{p}{p-1}\frac{-\gamma}{3}<1
, for any
\lambda<3+\gamma
and for any
t\geq 0,x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}|u-v|^{\gamma}f(u)\textnormal{d}u\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{1}_{v}}+\|f\|_{L^{p}_{v}}\lesssim(1+t)^{-\lambda}\|\langle v\rangle^{5}\langle x-t\hat{v}\rangle^{4}f\|_{L^{\infty}_{v}}.

Proof.

Denote
{p}^{\prime}=\frac{p}{p-1}
, then we have
p^{\prime}\gamma>-3
, thus using Hölder inequality we easily compute that

\int_{|u-v|\geq 1}|u-v|^{\gamma}f(u)\textnormal{d}u\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{1}_{v}},\quad\int_{|u-v|\leq 1}|u-v|^{\gamma}f(v)\textnormal{d}v\lesssim\int_{|u-v|\leq 1}|u-v|^{p^{\prime}\gamma}\textnormal{d}u\|f\|_{L^{p}_{v}}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{p}_{v}},

the proof is thus finished by using Lemma
2.5
.
∎

Lemma 2.7
.

Suppose
f
is a smooth function in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
such that

|f(x)|\lesssim K(1+|x|)^{-k}
for some constant
K>0,k\geq 3
, then for any
t\geq 0,x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
we have

\int_{|x-y|=t}f(y)\textnormal{d}S_{y}\lesssim t^{i}(1+t+|x|)^{-1}(1+|t-|x||)^{-(k-2)},\quad i=1,2.

(2.8)

where the constant is independent of
x,t,y
.

Proof.

The case
i=1
follows from
[
56
]
, Lemma 4.1. For the case
i=2
, when
t\geq\frac{1}{2}
, it is direct from
i=1
. When
t\leq\frac{1}{2}
, we have
|y|\geq|x|-\frac{1}{2}
, which implies that
\langle x\rangle\leq 2\langle y\rangle
and
3\langle x\rangle\geq 1+t+|x|
, thus we compute that

\int_{|x-y|=t}f(y)\textnormal{d}S_{y}\lesssim t^{2}\langle x\rangle^{-k}\lesssim t^{2}(1+t+|x|)^{-k},

so the lemma is thus proved.
∎

Lemma 2.8
.

For any
t\geq 0,x,v\in\mathbb{R}^{3},c\geq 1
.
Then we have that

|v\cdot e_{2}^{\prime}|+|v\cdot e_{3}^{\prime}|\lesssim|v\times\frac{x}{r}|,\quad\frac{|v\times\frac{x}{r}|}{v_{0}}\lesssim\sqrt{\kappa(v)},\quad\frac{c}{v_{0}}\lesssim\sqrt{\kappa(v)},

(2.9)

and

1+t+|x|\lesssim(1+|t-\frac{|x|}{c}|)\langle v\rangle^{4}\langle x-t\hat{v}\rangle^{2}.

(2.10)

Proof.

We compute that

2\kappa(v)v_{0}^{2}\geq\kappa(v)v_{0}(v_{0}+\frac{v\cdot x}{r})=(v_{0}-\frac{v\cdot x}{r})(v_{0}+\frac{v\cdot x}{r})=v_{0}^{2}-|v\cdot\frac{x}{r}|^{2}=c^{2}+|v\times\frac{x}{r}|^{2},

(2.11)

which implies that
\frac{|v\times\frac{x}{r}|}{v_{0}},\frac{c}{v_{0}}\lesssim\sqrt{\kappa(v)}
. Together with that
v\cdot e_{1}^{\prime}=v\cdot\frac{x}{r}
proves (
2.9
). To prove (
2.10
), we first compute

t\kappa(v)v_{0}=tv_{0}-\frac{tv\cdot x}{r}=tv_{0}-\frac{r}{c}v_{0}+\frac{|x|^{2}}{rc}v_{0}-\frac{tv\cdot x}{r}=(t-\frac{r}{c})v_{0}+\frac{v_{0}}{cr}[|x|^{2}-\frac{ctv\cdot x}{v_{0}}]=(t-\frac{r}{c})v_{0}+\frac{v_{0}}{cr}x\cdot(x-t\hat{v}),

thus together with (
2.11
), we have that

c^{2}(1+t)\lesssim v_{0}^{2}\kappa(v)(1+t)\lesssim(1+|t-\frac{r}{c}|)v_{0}^{2}+\frac{v_{0}^{2}}{c}|x-t\hat{v}|\Longrightarrow(1+t)\lesssim(1+|t-\frac{r}{c}|)\frac{v_{0}^{2}}{c^{2}}\langle x-t\hat{v}\rangle\lesssim(1+|t-\frac{r}{c}|)\langle v\rangle^{2}\langle x-t\hat{v}\rangle.

(2.12)

We split the next step it into three cases.

1.

|\hat{v}|\leq 1
. We have
t+|x-\hat{v}t|\geq|x|
, thus

(1+t+|x|)\lesssim(1+t)(1+|x-t\hat{v}|)\lesssim(1+t)\langle x-t\hat{v}\rangle
.

2.

|\hat{v}|\geq 1,|x-t\hat{v}|\geq 3t|\hat{v}|
. We have
|x|\geq 2t|\hat{v}|
and thus
|x-t\hat{v}|\geq\frac{1}{2}|x|
. So

(1+t+|x|)\lesssim(1+t|\hat{v}|+|x|)\lesssim\langle x-t\hat{v}\rangle
.

3.

|\hat{v}|\geq 1,|x-t\hat{v}|\leq 3t|\hat{v}|
. Then
|x|\leq 4t|\hat{v}|
, which implies that

(1+t+|x|)\lesssim(1+t+t|\hat{v}|)\lesssim(1+t)\langle v\rangle^{2}
.

thus gathering all the cases, we have that

1+t+|x|\lesssim(1+t)\langle v\rangle^{2}\langle x-t\hat{v}\rangle,

(2.13)

and
(
2.10
) is thus proved by gathering
(
2.12
) and (
2.13
).
∎

Lemma 2.9
.

For any
t\geq 0,x,v\in\mathbb{R}^{3},c\geq 1
, if
|x|\geq ct
, then

1+t+|x|\lesssim\langle v\rangle^{2}\langle x-t\hat{v}\rangle.

(2.14)

Proof.

(
2.14
) is direct if
|x|\leq 1
since
1+t+|x|\lesssim 1
.
If
|x|\geq\max\{ct,1\}
, then

6\langle v\rangle^{2}\langle x-t\hat{v}\rangle\geq 6\langle v\rangle^{2}(|x|-t|\hat{v}|)\geq 6\langle v\rangle^{2}|x|(1-|\frac{v}{v_{0}}|)\geq 2\langle v\rangle^{2}(1-|\frac{v}{v_{0}}|)(1+ct+|x|)\geq 1+t+|x|,

and the proof of (
2.14
) is thus finished .
∎

2.3
Integral inequalities

In this subsection, we prove three integral bounds, which are the general versions of Lemma 5.10 to 5.12 of
[
5
]
for any
c\geq 1
. We first recall a change of variable on the sphere
|x-y|=\sigma
.

Lemma 2.10

(
[
22
]
, Lemma 6.5.2 )
.

For any
\sigma\geq 0,x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, for any smooth function
f
we have

\int_{|x-y|=\sigma}f(|y|)\textnormal{d}S_{y}=\frac{2\pi\sigma}{|x|}\int_{||x|-\sigma|}^{|x|+\sigma}yf(y)\textnormal{d}y.

This could implies that

Lemma 2.11
.

For any smooth function
\mathpzc g
and any function
\mathpzc h
, for any
t,\sigma\geq 0,c\geq 1,x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, we have that

\displaystyle\int_{|y-x|\leq ct}\mathpzc g(t-\frac{1}{c}|y-x|,|y|)\mathpzc h(|x-y|)\textnormal{d}y=

\displaystyle c\frac{2\pi}{|x|}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{||x|-c(t-z)|}^{|x|+c(t-z)}y\mathpzc g(z,y)\textnormal{d}yc(t-z)\mathpzc h(c(t-z))\textnormal{d}z

(2.15)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c\frac{2\pi}{|x|}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{||x|-cz^{\prime}|}^{|x|+cz^{\prime}}y\mathpzc g(t-z^{\prime},y)\textnormal{d}ycz^{\prime}\mathpzc h(cz^{\prime})\textnormal{d}z^{\prime}.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.10
we have that

\displaystyle\textnormal{l.h.s. of }\eqref{eq:changevariable1}=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{ct}\int_{|x-y|=\tau}\mathpzc g(t-\frac{1}{c}\tau,|y|)\textnormal{d}S_{y}\mathpzc h(\tau)\textnormal{d}\tau=c\int_{0}^{t}\int_{|x-y|=c\tau_{1}}\mathpzc g(t-\tau_{1},|y|)\textnormal{d}S_{y}\mathpzc h(c\tau_{1})\textnormal{d}\tau_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c\int_{0}^{t}\int_{|x-y|=c(t-z)}\mathpzc g(z,|y|)\textnormal{d}S_{y}\mathpzc h(c(t-z))\textnormal{d}z=c\frac{2\pi}{|x|}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{||x|-c(t-z)|}^{|x|+c(t-z)}y\mathpzc g(z,y)\textnormal{d}yc(t-z)\mathpzc h(c(t-z))\textnormal{d}z

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textnormal{r.h.s. of }\eqref{eq:changevariable1},

so the proof is thus finished.
∎

Next, we list some inequalities that will be used later.

Lemma 2.12
.

For any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{3},c\geq 1,t\geq 0
that satisfy
\left|x\right|\leq\frac{1}{4}t,|y-x|\leq ct
, we have

|y-x|+1+t\lesssim 1+t-\frac{|y-x|}{c}+|y|.

(2.16)

Proof.

If
|y-x|\leq\frac{c}{2}t
, then
t-\frac{|y-x|}{c}\geq\frac{t}{2}
, and
|y-x|+1+t\lesssim|y|+|x|+1+t\lesssim 1+t-\frac{|y-x|}{c}+|y|
. If
|y-x|\in[\frac{c}{2}t,ct]
, we deduce (
2.16
) from
|y|\geq|y-x|-|x|\geq\frac{c}{4}t\geq\frac{1}{4}|y-x|
and
c\geq 1
.
∎

Lemma 2.13
.

We have the following integral inequalities.

•

For any
\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{c}\in\mathbb{R},\mathfrak{a}\leq\mathfrak{b}
, we have

\int_{\mathfrak{a}}^{\mathfrak{b}}\frac{1}{1+|z-\mathfrak{c}|}\textnormal{d}z\lesssim\log(1+|\mathfrak{a}-\mathfrak{c}|)+\log(1+|\mathfrak{b}-\mathfrak{c}|).

(2.17)

•

Given
N>1
. For any

\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{c}>0
,

\int_{0}^{\mathfrak{b}}\frac{1}{{\mathfrak{a}}^{N}+\mathfrak{c}^{N}z^{N}}\textnormal{d}z\lesssim_{N}\frac{1}{\mathfrak{a}^{N-1}\mathfrak{c}}.

(2.18)

•

For any
\mathfrak{a}>0
, we have

\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{z\left(z+\mathfrak{a}\right)^{3}}\textnormal{d}\mathpzc z<\frac{\log\left(1+\mathfrak{a}\right)}{\mathfrak{a}^{3}}.

(2.19)

Proof.

Equation

2.17
is obvious.
Equation

2.18
can be obtained from

\int_{0}^{\mathfrak{b}}\frac{1}{{\mathfrak{a}}^{N}+\mathfrak{c}^{N}z^{N}}\textnormal{d}z=\frac{1}{\mathfrak{c}}\int_{0}^{\mathfrak{c}\mathfrak{b}}\frac{1}{\mathfrak{a}^{N}+w^{N}}\textnormal{d}u=\frac{1}{\mathfrak{a}^{N-1}\mathfrak{c}}\int_{0}^{\frac{\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{c}}{\mathfrak{a}}}\frac{1}{1+y^{N-1}}\textnormal{d}y\lesssim_{N}\frac{1}{\mathfrak{a}^{N-1}\mathfrak{c}}.

(2.20)

and
Equation

2.19
is the direct result from

\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{z\left(z+\mathfrak{a}\right)^{3}}\textnormal{d}z=\frac{\log\left(1+\mathfrak{a}\right)}{\mathfrak{a}^{3}}-\frac{1}{\mathfrak{a}^{2}\left(1+\mathfrak{a}\right)}-\frac{1}{2\mathfrak{a}\left(1+\mathfrak{a}\right)^{2}}<\frac{\log\left(1+\mathfrak{a}\right)}{\mathfrak{a}^{3}}.

∎

Now we are ready to prove three integral bounds.

Lemma 2.14
.

For any
t\geq 0,x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{3},c\geq 1,a>3
, we have that

\mathcal{I}_{1}=\int_{|y-x|\leq ct}\frac{1}{(1+t-\frac{1}{c}|y-x|+|y|)^{a}(1+\left|t-\frac{1}{c}|y-x|-\frac{1}{c}|y|\right|)}\frac{1}{|x-y|}dy\lesssim\frac{c\log(3+|t-\frac{|x|}{c}|)}{(1+t+|x|)(1+|t-\frac{|x|}{c}|)^{a-2}}.

(2.21)

Proof.

First, by choosing
\mathpzc g\left(x,y\right)=\frac{1}{\left(1+x+y\right)^{a}\left(1+\left|x-\frac{y}{c}\right|\right)},\mathpzc h\left(x\right)=\frac{1}{x}
in Lemma
2.11
, we compute that

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{1}=

\displaystyle c\frac{2\pi}{|x|}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{||x|-c(t-z)|}^{|x|+c(t-z)}y\mathpzc g(z,y)\textnormal{d}yc(t-z)\mathpzc h(c(t-z))\textnormal{d}z\leq c\frac{2\pi}{|x|}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{||x|-c(t-z)|}^{|x|+c(t-z)}(1+z+y)^{1-a}\left(1+\left|z-\frac{y}{c}\right|\right)^{-1}\textnormal{d}y\textnormal{d}z.

We split it into two cases.

1.

|x|\leq\frac{1}{4}t
. Remind
y\geq||x|-c(t-z)|,\,\,z\geq 0
, we have

1+z+y\geq 1+t-\frac{|x|}{c}\geq 1+\frac{1}{2}t\geq\frac{1}{4}(1+t+|x|)
. So

\displaystyle(1+t+|x|)^{a-1}\mathcal{I}_{1}\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{c}{|x|}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{||x|-c(t-z)|}^{|x|+c(t-z)}\left(1+\left|z-\frac{y}{c}\right|\right)^{-1}\textnormal{d}y\textnormal{d}z

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{c^{2}}{|x|}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{|\frac{|x|}{c}-(t-z)|}^{\frac{|x|}{c}+t-z}(1+|z-z^{\prime}|)^{-1}\textnormal{d}z^{\prime}\textnormal{d}z=\frac{c^{2}}{|x|}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{|\frac{|x|}{c}-y|}^{\frac{|x|}{c}+y}(1+|t-y-z^{\prime}|)^{-1}\textnormal{d}z^{\prime}\textnormal{d}y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{c^{2}}{|x|}\int_{0}^{\frac{|x|}{c}}\int_{\frac{|x|}{c}-y}^{\frac{|x|}{c}+y}(1+|t-y-z^{\prime}|)^{-1}\textnormal{d}z^{\prime}\textnormal{d}y+\frac{c^{2}}{|x|}\int_{\frac{|x|}{c}}^{t}\int_{y-\frac{|x|}{c}}^{\frac{|x|}{c}+y}(1+|t-y-z^{\prime}|)^{-1}\textnormal{d}z^{\prime}\textnormal{d}y\vcentcolon=T_{1}+T_{2}.

Recall
Equation

2.17
, we have

T_{1}\lesssim c\log(3+t+\frac{|x|}{c})\lesssim c\log(3+|t-\frac{|x|}{c}|),

(2.22)

and

\displaystyle T_{2}=

\displaystyle\frac{c^{2}}{|x|}\int_{\frac{|x|}{c}}^{t}\int_{0}^{\frac{2|x|}{c}}(1+|t+\frac{|x|}{c}-2y-z|)^{-1}\textnormal{d}z\textnormal{d}y\lesssim c\int_{\frac{|x|}{c}}^{t}\int_{0}^{1}\left(1+\left|t+\frac{|x|}{c}-2y-2\frac{|x|}{c}z\right|\right)^{-1}\textnormal{d}z\textnormal{d}y

(2.23)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\frac{|x|}{c}}^{t}\left(1+\left|t+\frac{|x|}{c}-2y-2\frac{|x|}{c}z\right|\right)^{-1}\textnormal{d}y\textnormal{d}z\lesssim c\log(3+t)\lesssim c\log(3+|t-\frac{|x|}{c}|).

2.

|x|>\frac{1}{4}t
. Let
\mathfrak{u}=z+y,\mathfrak{v}=z-y
, then
z-\frac{y}{c}=\frac{\mathfrak{u}+\mathfrak{v}}{2}-\frac{\mathfrak{u}-\mathfrak{v}}{2c}=\frac{c-1}{2c}\mathfrak{u}+\frac{c+1}{2c}\mathfrak{v}.

From direct calculations, we have

\min_{z\in[0,t]}||x|-c(t-z)|+z=\begin{cases}t-\frac{|x|}{c},\quad\hbox{if}\quad|x|\leq ct,\\
|x-ct|,\quad\hbox{if}\quad|x|\geq ct\end{cases}.

Since
z,y\geq 0
, we have
-\mathfrak{u}\leq\mathfrak{v}\leq\mathfrak{u}
, and

{t-\frac{|x|}{c}}\leq z+|x-c(t-z)|\leq\mathfrak{u}\leq\max_{z\in[0,t]}\{z+|x|+c(t-z)\}\leq|x|+ct
. Together with
Equation

2.17
and
a>3
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{1}\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{c}{|x|}\int_{\left|t-\frac{|x|}{c}\right|}^{x+ct}\int_{-\mathfrak{u}}^{\mathfrak{u}}(1+\mathfrak{u})^{1-a}\left(1+\left|\frac{c-1}{2c}\mathfrak{u}+\frac{c+1}{2c}\mathfrak{v}\right|\right)^{-1}\textnormal{d}\mathfrak{v}\textnormal{d}\mathfrak{u}\lesssim\frac{c}{|x|}\int_{\left|t-\frac{|x|}{c}\right|}^{x+ct}\int_{-2\mathfrak{u}}^{2\mathfrak{u}}(1+\mathfrak{u})^{1-a}\left(1+\left|\frac{c-1}{c+1}\mathfrak{u}+\mathfrak{v}\right|\right)^{-1}\textnormal{d}\mathfrak{v}\textnormal{d}\mathfrak{u}

(2.24)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{c}{|x|}\int_{\left|t-\frac{|x|}{c}\right|}^{x+ct}(1+\mathfrak{u})^{1-a}\log(3+\mathfrak{u})\textnormal{d}\mathfrak{u}\lesssim\frac{c}{|x|}\frac{\log(3+|t-\frac{|x|}{c}|)}{(1+|t-\frac{|x|}{c}|)^{a-3}}\int_{\left|t-\frac{|x|}{c}\right|}^{x+ct}(1+\mathfrak{u})^{-2}\textnormal{d}\mathfrak{u}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{c}{|x|}\frac{\log(3+|t-\frac{|x|}{c}|)}{(1+|t-\frac{|x|}{c}|)^{a-2}}\frac{x+ct}{1+x+ct}\lesssim c\frac{\log(3+|t-\frac{|x|}{c}|)}{(1+t+|x|)(1+|t-\frac{|x|}{c}|)^{a-2}},

so (
2.21
) is thus proved by combining (
2.22
), (
2.23
), (
2.24
) together.
∎

Lemma 2.15
.

For any
t\geq 0,x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{3},c\geq 1,a\geq 3
we have that

\mathcal{I}_{2}=\int_{|y-x|\leq ct}\frac{1}{(1+t-\frac{1}{c}|y-x|+|y|)^{a}}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{2}}\textnormal{d}y\lesssim\frac{1}{(1+t+|x|)(1+|t-\frac{|x|}{c}|)^{a-2}},

(2.25)

where the constant is independent of
t,y,x,c
.

Proof.

First, for
\left|y-x\right|\leq ct
, we have that

t-\frac{1}{c}|y-x|+|y|\geq t-\frac{1}{c}|y|-\frac{1}{c}|x|+|y|\geq t-\frac{1}{c}|x|,\quad t-\frac{1}{c}|y-x|+|y|\geq|y|\geq|x|-|y-x|\geq|x|-ct.

The two inequalities imply that

1+t-\frac{1}{c}|y-x|+|y|\geq 1+|t-\frac{|x|}{c}|
. So we only need to prove (
2.25
) for
a=3
. We split the proof into several cases.

1.

If
ct\leq\frac{1}{2}
. For this case we have
|x|+1\lesssim|y|+1
, then we have that

\mathcal{I}_{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{(1+|x|)^{3}}\int_{|y-x|\leq 1}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{2}}\textnormal{d}y\lesssim\frac{1}{(1+t+|x|)^{3}}.

(2.26)

2.

If
|x|\leq\frac{1}{2}
. Then we have
|y-x|+1\lesssim|y|+1
, thus

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{2}\lesssim

\displaystyle\int_{|y-x|\leq ct}\frac{1}{(1+t+|y-x|)^{3}}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{2}}\textnormal{d}y=\int_{|z|\leq ct}\frac{1}{(1+t+|z|)^{3}}\frac{1}{|z|^{2}}\textnormal{d}z

(2.27)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{ct}\frac{1}{(1+t+|z|)^{3}}\textnormal{d}|z|\lesssim\frac{1}{(1+t)^{2}}\lesssim\frac{1}{(1+t+|x|)^{2}}.

3.

If
|x|\leq\frac{1}{4}t
. From Lemma
2.12
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{2}\lesssim

\displaystyle\int_{|y-x|\leq ct}\frac{1}{(1+t+|y-x|)^{3}}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{2}}\textnormal{d}y\lesssim\frac{1}{(1+t+|x|)^{2}}.

(2.28)

4.

If
|x|\geq ct
. We choose
\mathpzc g(x,y)=\frac{1}{(1+x+y)^{3}},\mathpzc h(x)=\frac{1}{x^{2}}
in (
2.15
). Remind that
z^{\prime}=t-\mathpzc z
, we have
|x|\geq cz^{\prime}
. So

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{2}=

\displaystyle c\frac{2\pi}{|x|}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{||x|-cz^{\prime}|}^{|x|+cz^{\prime}}y(1+t-z^{\prime}+y)^{-3}\textnormal{d}y\frac{1}{cz^{\prime}}\textnormal{d}z^{\prime}\leq c\frac{2\pi}{|x|}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{|x|-cz^{\prime}}^{|x|+cz^{\prime}}(1+t-z^{\prime}+y)^{-2}\textnormal{d}y\frac{1}{cz^{\prime}}\textnormal{d}z^{\prime}

(2.29)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c\frac{4\pi}{|x|}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{(1+t-z^{\prime}+|x|-cz^{\prime})(1+t-z^{\prime}+|x|+cz^{\prime})}\textnormal{d}z^{\prime}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(1+|x|-ct)(1+t+|x|)}\frac{c}{|x|}\int_{0}^{t}\textnormal{d}z^{\prime}\lesssim\frac{1}{(1+t+|x|)(1+|t-\frac{|x|}{c}|)}.

5.

If
\frac{1}{8}(1+t)\leq|x|\leq ct
. Remind that
\frac{1}{8}\left(1+t\right)\leq ct
if
ct>\frac{1}{2}
.
choose
N=2
in
Equation

2.18
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{2}\leq

\displaystyle c\frac{2\pi}{|x|}\int_{0}^{\frac{|x|}{c}}\int_{|x|-cz^{\prime}}^{|x|+cz^{\prime}}(1+t-z^{\prime}+y)^{-2}\textnormal{d}y\frac{1}{cz^{\prime}}\textnormal{d}z^{\prime}+c\frac{2\pi}{|x|}\int_{\frac{|x|}{c}}^{t}\int_{cz^{\prime}-|x|}^{|x|+cz^{\prime}}(1+t-z^{\prime}+y)^{-2}\textnormal{d}y\frac{1}{cz^{\prime}}\textnormal{d}z^{\prime}

(2.30)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4\pi\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{1+t-z^{\prime}+|x|+cz^{\prime}}\left(\frac{c\mathbbm{1}_{z^{\prime}\in[0,\frac{|x|}{c}]}}{|x|\left(1+t-z^{\prime}+|x|-cz^{\prime}\right)}+\frac{\mathbbm{1}_{z^{\prime}\in[\frac{|x|}{c},t]}}{z^{\prime}\left(1+t-z^{\prime}+cz^{\prime}-|x|\right)}\right)\textnormal{d}z^{\prime}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{c}{|x|}\int_{0}^{\frac{|x|}{c}}\frac{1}{(1+t-\frac{|x|}{c})(1+t+|x|)}\textnormal{d}z^{\prime}+\frac{1}{1+t+|x|}\int_{\frac{|x|}{c}}^{t}\frac{1}{(1+t-z^{\prime}+cz^{\prime}-|x|)z^{\prime}}\textnormal{d}z^{\prime}.

Thus for
c\geq 2
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{2}\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(1+t-\frac{|x|}{c})(1+t+|x|)}+\frac{1}{1+t+|x|}\int_{0}^{t-\frac{|x|}{c}}\frac{1}{(1+t-\frac{|x|}{c}+\frac{c}{2}z)(z+\frac{|x|}{c})}\textnormal{d}z

(2.31)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(1+t-\frac{|x|}{c})(1+t+|x|)}+\frac{1}{1+t+|x|}\int_{0}^{t-\frac{|x|}{c}}\frac{1}{(1+t-\frac{|x|}{c})\frac{|x|}{c}+cz^{2}}\textnormal{d}z

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(1+t-\frac{|x|}{c})(1+t+|x|)}+\frac{1}{1+t+|x|}\frac{1}{\sqrt{(1+t-\frac{|x|}{c})|x|}}\lesssim\frac{1}{(1+t-\frac{|x|}{c})(1+t+|x|)}.

For
c\in[1,2]
, from
\int_{0}^{t-\frac{|x|}{c}}\frac{1}{(1+t-\frac{|x|}{c})(z+\frac{|x|}{c})}\textnormal{d}z=\frac{1}{1+t-\frac{|x|}{c}}\log\left(\frac{ct}{\left|x\right|}\right)\lesssim\frac{1}{1+t-\frac{|x|}{c}}
, we still have

\mathcal{I}_{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{(1+t-\frac{|x|}{c})(1+t+|x|)}.

(2.32)

The proof is thus finished by gathering (
2.26
) to (
2.32
) together.
∎

Lemma 2.16
.

For any
t\geq 0,c\geq 1,ct\geq 1,x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, we have that

\mathcal{I}_{3}=\int_{1\leq|y-x|\leq ct}\frac{1}{(1+t-\frac{1}{c}|y-x|+|y|)^{3}}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{3}}\textnormal{d}y\lesssim\frac{\log(3+t+|x|)}{(1+t+|x|)^{2}(1+|t-\frac{|x|}{c}|)}.

(2.33)

Proof.

We split into different cases of
x
.

1.

If
|x|\leq\frac{1}{2}
. Then we have that
|y-x|+1\lesssim|y|+1
. We obtain from
Equation

2.19
that

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{3}\lesssim

\displaystyle\int_{1\leq|y-x|\leq ct}\frac{1}{(1+t+|y-x|)^{3}}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{3}}\textnormal{d}y=\int_{1\leq|z|\leq ct}\frac{1}{(1+t+|z|)^{3}}\frac{1}{|z|^{3}}\textnormal{d}|z|

(2.34)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\int_{1}^{ct}\frac{1}{|z|(1+t+|z|)^{3}}\textnormal{d}|z|\lesssim\frac{\log(3+t)}{(1+t)^{3}}\lesssim\frac{\log(3+t)}{(1+t+|x|)^{3}}.

2.

If
|x|\leq\frac{1}{4}t
. From Lemma
2.12
, we have

\mathcal{I}_{3}\lesssim\int_{|y-x|\leq ct}\frac{1}{(1+t+|y-x|)^{3}}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{2}}\textnormal{d}y\lesssim\frac{\log(3+t)}{(1+t+|x|)^{3}}.

(2.35)

3.

If
|x|\geq ct
. Choose
\mathpzc g\left(x,y\right)=\frac{1}{\left(1+x+y\right)^{3}},\mathpzc h\left(x\right)=\frac{1}{x^{3}}
in Lemma
2.11
, and remind that
|x|\geq cz^{\prime}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{3}=

\displaystyle c\frac{2\pi}{|x|}\int_{\frac{1}{c}}^{t}\int_{||x|-cz^{\prime}|}^{|x|+cz^{\prime}}y(1+t-z^{\prime}+y)^{-3}\frac{1}{c^{2}z^{\prime 2}}\textnormal{d}y\textnormal{d}z^{\prime}\leq c\frac{2\pi}{|x|}\int_{\frac{1}{c}}^{t}\int_{|x|-cz^{\prime}}^{|x|+cz^{\prime}}(1+t-z^{\prime}+y)^{-2}\textnormal{d}y\frac{1}{c^{2}z^{\prime 2}}\textnormal{d}z^{\prime}

(2.36)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c\frac{4\pi}{|x|}\int_{\frac{1}{c}}^{t}\frac{1}{(1+t-z^{\prime}+|x|-cz^{\prime})(1+t-z^{\prime}+|x|+cz^{\prime})}\frac{1}{cz^{\prime}}\textnormal{d}z^{\prime}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{c}{|x|}\frac{1}{(1+t+|x|)(1+|x|-ct)}\int_{\frac{1}{c}}^{t}\frac{1}{cz^{\prime}}\textnormal{d}z^{\prime}=\frac{1}{(1+t+|x|)(1+|x|-ct)}\frac{\log(3+ct)}{|x|}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{\log(3+t+|x|)}{(1+t+|x|)^{2}(|1+t-\frac{|x|}{c}|)}.

4.

If
\frac{1}{8}(1+t)\leq|x|\leq ct
. Similarly as (
2.30
),
we have

\mathcal{I}_{3}\lesssim\frac{1}{|x|}\int_{\frac{1}{c}}^{\frac{|x|}{c}}\frac{1}{(1+t-\frac{|x|}{c})(1+t+|x|)}\frac{1}{z^{\prime}}\textnormal{d}z^{\prime}+\frac{1}{1+t+|x|}\int_{\frac{|x|}{c}}^{t}\frac{1}{(1+t-z^{\prime}+cz^{\prime}-|x|)cz^{\prime 2}}\textnormal{d}z^{\prime}.

(2.37)

If
c\geq 2
, choose
N=3
in
Equation

2.18
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{3}\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|x|}\int_{\frac{1}{c}}^{\frac{|x|}{c}}\frac{1}{(1+t-\frac{|x|}{c})(1+t+|x|)}\frac{1}{z}^{\prime}\textnormal{d}z^{\prime}+\frac{1}{1+t+|x|}\int_{\frac{|x|}{c}}^{t}\frac{1}{(1+t-w+cz^{\prime}-|x|)cz^{\prime 2}}\textnormal{d}z^{\prime}

(2.38)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{\log(3+|x|)}{(1+t-\frac{|x|}{c})(1+t+|x|)^{2}}+\frac{1}{1+t+|x|}\int_{0}^{t-\frac{|x|}{c}}\frac{1}{(1+t-\frac{|x|}{c})\frac{|x|^{2}}{c}+c^{2}z^{3}}\textnormal{d}z

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{\log(3+|x|)}{(1+t-\frac{|x|}{c})(1+t+|x|)^{2}}+\frac{1}{1+t+|x|}\frac{1}{((1+t-\frac{|x|}{c})^{2}|x|^{4})^{\frac{1}{3}}}\lesssim\frac{\log(3+|x|)}{(1+t-\frac{|x|}{c})(1+t+|x|)^{2}}.

If
c\in[1,2]
, from
\int_{\frac{|x|}{c}}^{t}\frac{1}{cz^{\prime 2}}\textnormal{d}z^{\prime}=\frac{ct-|x|}{ct|x|}\lesssim\frac{\log(3+|x|)}{1+t+|x|}
, we still have

\mathcal{I}_{3}\lesssim\frac{\log(3+|x|)}{(1+t-\frac{|x|}{c})(1+t+|x|)^{2}}.

(2.39)

The proof is thus finished by combining (
2.34
) to (
2.39
) together.
∎

2.4
Commutation vector fields

In this subsection we elaborate on the commutators for the Maxwell equations and the ones for the relativistic Vlasov part of the equation.

Lemma 2.17
.

For any
Z_{1},Z_{2}\in Z
and
\hat{Z}^{1},\hat{Z}^{2}\in\hat{Z}
we have that

[Z_{1},Z_{2}]=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq 1}C_{\alpha}Z^{\alpha},\quad[\hat{Z}^{1},\hat{Z}^{2}]=\sum_{|\beta|\leq 1}C_{\beta}\hat{Z}^{\beta},

where
C_{\alpha},C_{\beta}
is uniformly (in
t,x,c
) bounded from above.(May be a linear combination of
\frac{1}{c}
.)

Proof.

We first compute the commutators. We have

\displaystyle[\partial_{t},\hat{\Omega}_{jk}]=0,\,\,[\partial_{x_{i}},\hat{\Omega}_{jk}]=\delta_{ij}\partial_{x_{k}}-\delta_{ik}\partial_{x_{j}},\,\,[\partial_{t},\Omega_{j}]=\partial_{x_{j}},\,\,[\partial_{x_{i}},\Omega_{j}]=\frac{1}{c^{2}}\delta_{ij}\partial_{t},\,\,[\frac{1}{c}\partial_{t},S]=\frac{1}{c^{2}}\partial_{t},\,\,[\partial_{x_{i}},S]=\partial_{x_{i}},

(2.40)

\displaystyle[\hat{\Omega}_{i},S]=[\hat{\Omega}_{jk},S]=0,\,\,[\hat{\Omega}_{i},\hat{\Omega}_{j}]=\frac{1}{c^{2}}\hat{\Omega}_{ij},\,\,[\hat{\Omega}_{i},\hat{\Omega}_{jk}]=\delta_{ij}\hat{\Omega}_{k}-\delta_{ik}\hat{\Omega}_{j},\,\,[\Omega_{i},\Omega_{jk}]=\delta_{ij}\Omega_{k}-\delta_{ik}\Omega_{j},

\displaystyle[\Omega_{jk},\Omega_{il})]=\delta_{jl}\Omega_{ki}+\delta_{ik}\Omega_{jl}+\delta_{kl}\Omega_{ij}+\delta_{ij}\Omega_{lk},\,\,[\hat{\Omega}_{jk},\hat{\Omega}_{il}]=\delta_{jl}\hat{\Omega}_{ki}+\delta_{ik}\hat{\Omega}_{jl}+\delta_{kl}\hat{\Omega}_{ij}+\delta_{ij}\hat{\Omega}_{lk},

and the lemma is thus proved by (
2.40
).
∎

Lemma 2.18
.

Suppose that
[f,E,B]
solves the relativistic Vlasov-Maxwell-Boltzmann equation (
1.1
), (
1.2
). Denote that

h^{i}_{1}(f):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left(c^{2}\partial_{x_{i}}+\frac{cv_{i}}{v_{0}}\partial_{t}\right)f\textnormal{d}v,\quad h^{j,k}_{2}(f):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left(\frac{c^{2}v_{j}}{v_{0}}\partial_{x_{k}}-\frac{c^{2}v_{k}}{v_{0}}\partial_{x_{j}}\right)f\textnormal{d}v,

then there exist uniformly bounded constants
C_{\alpha,\beta,i}
and
C_{\alpha,\beta,j,k}
, such that

(\partial_{t}^{2}-{{c^{2}}}\Delta_{x})(Z^{\alpha}[E,B])_{l}=\sum_{\beta\leq\alpha}\sum_{i=1}^{3}C_{\alpha,\beta,i,l}h^{i}_{1}(\hat{Z}^{\beta}f)+\sum_{\beta\leq\alpha}\sum_{j,k=1}^{3}C_{\alpha,\beta,j,k,l}h^{j,k}_{2}(\hat{Z}^{\beta}f).

(2.41)

where
l=1,2,...,6
.

Proof.

Taking another
t
derivative on (
1.2
), using that
\nabla\times(\nabla\times f)=\nabla(\nabla\cdot f)-\Delta f

we have that

\partial_{t}^{2}E=-c^{2}\nabla\times(\nabla\times E)-\partial_{t}J=c^{2}\Delta E-c^{2}\nabla J_{0}-\partial_{t}J,\quad\partial_{t}^{2}B=-c^{2}\nabla\times(\nabla\times B)+c\nabla\times J=c^{2}\Delta B+c\nabla\times J,

(2.42)

and

(c^{2}\nabla_{x}J_{0}+\partial_{t}J)_{i}=h^{i}_{1}(f),\quad c\nabla\times J=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(\frac{c^{2}v_{i+2}}{v_{0}}\partial_{x_{i+1}}-\frac{c^{2}v_{i+1}}{v_{0}}\partial_{x_{i+2}})f\textnormal{d}v=h_{2}^{i+2,i+1}(f),\quad\text{where}\quad v_{i+3}=v_{i},\,\,x_{i+3}=x_{i},\,\,i=1,2.

We next show that the commutation vector field commutes with the wave equation. From

[\partial_{t}^{2}-c^{2}\Delta_{x},Z]=0,\quad Z\in\{\frac{1}{c}\partial_{t},\partial_{x_{i}},\Omega_{ij},\Omega_{i}\},\quad[\partial_{t}^{2}-c^{2}\Delta_{x},S]=\frac{2}{c}(\partial_{t}^{2}-c^{2}\Delta_{x}).

Combine the estimates of commutators above together and remind (
2.42
), we have

(\partial_{t}^{2}-{{c^{2}}}\Delta_{x})(Z^{\alpha}[E,B])_{l}=\sum_{\beta\leq\alpha}C_{\alpha,\beta}Z^{\beta}(\partial_{t}^{2}-{{c^{2}}}\Delta_{x})([E,B])_{l}=\sum_{\beta\leq\alpha}\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{3}C_{\alpha,\beta,i,l}Z^{\beta}h_{1}^{i}(f).+C_{\alpha,\beta,j,k,l}h_{2}^{j,k}(f)

(2.43)

We next calculate the right hand side of (
2.43
).

1.

From integration by parts, we compute that

\displaystyle h^{i}_{1}\left(\hat{\Omega}_{j}f\right)=

\displaystyle\Omega_{j}\left(h^{i}_{1}(f)\right)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(\delta_{ij}\partial_{t}+\frac{cv_{i}}{v_{0}}\partial_{x_{j}})f\textnormal{d}v+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(c^{2}\partial_{x_{i}}+\frac{cv_{i}}{v_{0}}\partial_{t})(\frac{v_{0}}{c}\partial_{v_{j}})f\textnormal{d}v

(2.44)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Omega_{j}\left(h^{i}_{1}(f)\right)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(\delta_{ij}\partial_{t}+\frac{cv_{i}}{v_{0}}\partial_{x_{j}})f\textnormal{d}v-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(\delta_{ij}\partial_{t}+\frac{cv_{j}}{v_{0}}\partial_{x_{i}})f\textnormal{d}v

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Omega_{j}\left(h^{i}_{1}(f)\right)+\frac{1}{c}h^{i,j}_{2}(f),

and

\displaystyle h^{i}_{1}\left(\hat{\Omega}_{jk}f\right)=

\displaystyle\Omega_{jk}\left(h^{i}_{1}(f)\right)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}c^{2}(\delta_{ij}\partial_{x_{k}}-\delta_{ik}\partial_{x_{j}})f\textnormal{d}v+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(c^{2}\partial_{x_{i}}+\frac{cv_{i}}{v_{0}}\partial_{t})(v_{j}\partial_{v_{k}}-v_{k}\partial_{v_{j}})f\textnormal{d}v

(2.45)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Omega_{jk}\left(h^{i}_{1}(f)\right)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}c^{2}(\delta_{ij}\partial_{x_{k}}-\delta_{ik}\partial_{x_{j}})f\textnormal{d}v+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(-\delta_{ik}\frac{cv_{j}}{v_{0}}+\delta_{ij}\frac{cv_{k}}{v_{0}})\partial_{t}f\textnormal{d}v

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Omega_{jk}\left(h^{i}_{1}(f)\right)-\delta_{ik}h^{j}_{1}(f)+\delta_{ij}h^{k}_{1}(f),

h^{i}_{1}\left(Sf\right)=S\left(h^{i}_{1}(f)\right)+\frac{1}{c}h^{i}_{1}(f).

(2.46)

2.

Similarly as (
2.44
) to (
2.46
), we have

h^{i,j}_{2}\left(\hat{\Omega}_{k}f\right)=\Omega_{k}\left(h^{i,j}_{2}(f)\right)+\frac{1}{c}\left(-\delta_{ik}h^{j}_{1}(f)+\delta_{jk}h^{i}_{1}(f)\right),

(2.47)

h^{i,j}_{2}\left(\hat{\Omega}_{lk}f\right)=\Omega_{lk}\left(h^{ij}_{2}(f)\right)+\delta_{ik}h^{j,l}_{2}(f)+\delta_{jk}h^{l,i}_{2}(f)+\delta_{jl}h^{i,k}_{2}(f)+\delta_{il}h^{k,j}_{2}(f),

(2.48)

h^{i,j}_{2}\left(Sf\right)=S\left(h^{i,j}_{2}(f)\right)+\frac{1}{c}h^{i,j}_{2}(f).

(2.49)

Equations (
2.44
)- (
2.49
) imply that there exist uniformly bounded constants
C_{\alpha,\beta,i}
and
C_{\alpha,\beta,j,k}
, such that

Z^{\alpha}h^{i}_{1}(f),\quad Z^{\alpha}h^{j,k}_{2}(f)=\sum_{\beta\leq\alpha}C_{\alpha,\beta,i}h^{i}_{1}(\hat{Z}^{\beta}f)+\sum_{\beta\leq\alpha}C_{\alpha,\beta,j,k}h^{j,k}_{2}(\hat{Z}^{\beta}f),

(2.50)

and (
2.41
) is proved after gathering (
2.43
) and (
2.50
).
∎

Lemma 2.19
.

For the
e_{i}^{\prime}
defined in (
2.4
), there exist uniformly bounded constants
C_{i,j,k}(\theta,\phi)
, such that

e_{1}^{\prime}\times\nabla=\frac{(\Omega_{23},\Omega_{31},\Omega_{12})}{r},\quad e_{i}^{\prime}\cdot\nabla=\frac{1}{r}\sum_{j,k=1,j<k}^{3}C_{i,j,k}(\theta,\phi)\Omega_{jk},\quad i=2,3.

(2.51)

Proof.

Remind (
2.5
), we have
e_{1}^{\prime}\times\nabla=\frac{1}{r}(x\times\nabla_{x})
, thus the first equality is proved. The second equality is the direct result of

e_{2}^{\prime}\cdot\nabla=(e_{3}^{\prime}\times e_{1}^{\prime})\cdot\nabla=e_{3}^{\prime}\cdot(e_{1}^{\prime}\times\nabla),\quad e_{3}^{\prime}\cdot\nabla=(e_{1}^{\prime}\times e_{2}^{\prime})\cdot\nabla=-e_{2}^{\prime}\cdot(e_{1}^{\prime}\times\nabla).

∎

Lemma 2.20
.

For any vector function
E
, for any
j=1,2,3
we have the following identities

\partial_{x_{j}}E\cdot e_{1}^{\prime}=\frac{1}{r}\partial_{x_{j}}E\cdot x=\frac{x_{j}}{r}(\nabla\cdot E)+\frac{1}{r}\sum_{i\neq j}\Omega_{ij}E_{i},

(2.52)

\partial_{r}(E)\cdot e_{1}^{\prime}=\nabla\cdot E+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\sum_{i,j,i\neq j}x_{j}\Omega_{ij}E_{i}=\nabla\cdot E+\frac{1}{r}\sum_{i,j}C_{i,j}(\theta,\phi)\Omega_{ij}E_{i},

(2.53)

and

(\nabla\times E)\cdot e_{3}^{\prime}-\partial_{r}(E)\cdot e_{2}^{\prime}=-e_{1}^{\prime}\cdot(e_{2}^{\prime}\cdot\nabla)E,\quad(\nabla\times E)\cdot e_{2}^{\prime}+\partial_{r}(E)\cdot e_{3}^{\prime}=e_{1}^{\prime}\cdot(e_{3}^{\prime}\cdot\nabla)E.

(2.54)

Proof.

For any
j=1,2,3
we compute that

\partial_{x_{j}}E\cdot x=x_{j}\partial_{x_{j}}E_{j}+\sum_{i\neq j}\partial_{x_{j}}E_{i}x_{i}=x_{j}\sum_{i=1}^{3}\partial_{x_{i}}E_{i}-x_{j}\sum_{i\neq j}\partial_{x_{i}}E_{i}+\sum_{i\neq j}\partial_{x_{j}}E_{i}x_{i}=x_{j}(\nabla\cdot E)+\sum_{i\neq j}\Omega_{ij}E_{i}.

(2.55)

Thus taking the summation, we have that

\partial_{r}(E\cdot e_{1}^{\prime})=\frac{1}{r}\sum_{j}x_{j}\partial_{x_{j}}E\cdot\frac{x}{r}=\frac{1}{r^{2}}[\sum_{j}x_{j}\partial_{x_{j}}E\cdot x]=\frac{1}{r^{2}}[\sum_{j}|x_{j}|^{2}(\nabla\cdot E)+x_{j}\sum_{i\neq j}\Omega_{ij}E_{i}]=\nabla\cdot E+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\sum_{i,j,i\neq j}x_{j}\Omega_{ij}E_{i},

(2.56)

and (
2.52
), (
2.53
) can be obtained from (
2.55
), (
2.56
).
For (
2.54
),
remind (
2.6a
),
we have that

(\nabla\times E)\cdot e_{3}^{\prime}-\partial_{r}(E)\cdot e_{2}^{\prime}=(e_{1}^{\prime}\times e_{2}^{\prime})\cdot(\nabla\times E)-e_{2}^{\prime}\cdot(e_{1}^{\prime}\cdot\nabla)E=e_{2}^{\prime}\cdot(e_{1}^{\prime}\cdot\nabla)E-e_{1}^{\prime}\cdot(e_{2}^{\prime}\cdot\nabla)E-e_{2}^{\prime}\cdot(e_{1}^{\prime}\cdot\nabla)E=-e_{1}^{\prime}\cdot(e_{2}^{\prime}\cdot\nabla)E,

and the other equality in (
2.54
) can be proved similarly, so the proof is thus finished.
∎

Next, we show that

Lemma 2.21
.

For
\xi=\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\rho,\sigma
, suppose that
[f,E,B]
satisfies the Vlasov-Maxwell-Boltzmann equation (
1.1
), (
1.2
) and
J_{i}
is defined in (
1.3
). Then we have

(\frac{1}{c}\partial_{t}-\partial_{r})\xi([E,B])=\sum_{i=0}^{3}C_{i}(\theta,\phi)J_{i}(f)+\frac{1}{r}\sum_{|\gamma|\leq 1}\sum_{i=1}^{3}C_{\gamma,i,1}(\theta,\phi)Z^{\gamma}E_{i}+C_{\gamma,i,2}(\theta,\phi)Z^{\gamma}B_{i},

where the constants are uniformly bounded from above.

Proof.

First we compute that

\partial_{t}(\rho[E,B])=e_{1}^{\prime}\cdot(\partial_{t}E)=ce_{1}^{\prime}\cdot(\nabla\times B)+e_{1}^{\prime}\cdot J=c(e_{1}^{\prime}\times\nabla)\cdot B+e_{1}^{\prime}\cdot J,

also by (
2.53
),

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\rho=\partial_{r}(E)\cdot e_{1}^{\prime}=\nabla\cdot E+\frac{1}{r}\sum_{i,j}C_{i,j}(\theta,\phi)\Omega_{ij}E_{i}=J_{0}+\frac{1}{r}\sum_{i,j}C_{i,j}(\theta,\phi)\Omega_{ij}E_{i}.

Thus by Lemma
2.19
the case
\xi=\rho
is proved. The case
\xi=\sigma
is similar. For
\xi=\alpha_{2}
, from (
2.54
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c}\partial_{t}(\alpha_{1}[E,B])-\partial_{r}(\alpha_{1}[E,B])=

\displaystyle-(\nabla\times E)\cdot e_{2}^{\prime}+(\nabla\times B)\cdot e_{3}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{c}J\cdot e_{3}^{\prime}-\partial_{r}(B\cdot e_{2}^{\prime}+E\cdot e_{3}^{\prime})=e_{1}^{\prime}\cdot(e_{3}^{\prime}\cdot\nabla)E+e_{1}^{\prime}\cdot(e_{2}^{\prime}\cdot\nabla)B,

so by using Lemma
2.19
we have finished the proof for
\alpha_{1}
. Similarly for
\xi=\alpha_{2}
, we compute that

\frac{1}{c}\partial_{t}(\alpha_{2}[E,B])-\partial_{r}(\alpha_{2}[E,B])=-e_{2}^{\prime}\cdot(e_{3}^{\prime}\cdot\nabla)B+e_{1}^{\prime}\cdot(e_{2}^{\prime}\cdot\nabla)E,

so the lemma is thus proved.
∎

Lemma 2.22
.

For
\xi=\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\rho,\sigma
and
Z=S,\Omega_{i},\Omega_{ij}(1\leq i,j\leq 3)
, suppose that
[f,E,B]
satisfies the Vlasov-Maxwell-Boltzmann equation (
1.1
), (
1.2
) and
J
is defined in (
1.3
). Then

(\frac{1}{c}\partial_{t}-\partial_{r})\xi(\mathpzc D_{Z}([E,B]))=\frac{1}{r}\left(\sum_{|\alpha|\leq 2}\sum_{i=1}^{3}C_{\alpha,i,1}(\theta,\phi)Z^{\alpha}E_{i}+C_{\alpha,i,2}(\theta,\phi)Z^{\alpha}B_{i}\right)+\sum_{|\beta|\leq 1}\sum_{i=0}^{3}C_{\beta,i}(\theta,\phi)J_{i}(\hat{Z}^{\beta}f),

where the constants are uniformly bounded from above.

Proof.

We split into three different cases of
Z
.

1.

If
{Z}=S
. Then
\mathpzc D_{Z}([E,B])=Z[E,B]
, and
[\partial_{t}-c\partial_{r},S]=\frac{1}{c}(\partial_{t}-c\partial_{r})
. Since
S
only contains
\partial_{r},\partial_{t}
, which commutes with
e_{2}^{\prime},e_{3}^{\prime},e_{1}^{\prime}
, thus commutes with
\xi
,

\displaystyle(\frac{1}{c}\partial_{t}-\partial_{r})[\xi(S[E,B])]]=(\frac{1}{c}\partial_{t}-\partial_{r})S[\xi([E,B])]]=S(\frac{1}{c}\partial_{t}-\partial_{r})[\xi([E,B])]+\frac{1}{c}(\frac{1}{c}\partial_{t}-\partial_{r})[\xi([E,B])].

Together with Lemma
2.21
and the fact that
S
commutes with
\theta,\phi
, the case
{Z}=S
is thus proved.

2.

If
Z=\Omega
.
We prove the case
Z={\Omega}_{1}
and
{\Omega}_{2},{\Omega}_{3}
can be proved similarly.

Case 1:
\xi=\rho,\sigma
.
We only proof the
\rho
term as the
\sigma
term is similar. Recall that
Z={\Omega}_{1}=t\partial_{x_{1}}+\frac{1}{c^{2}}x_{1}\partial_{t}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathpzc\displaystyle D^{(1)}_{Z}(E,B)=ZE+\frac{1}{c}(0,-B_{3},B_{2})=ZE+\frac{1}{c}e_{1}\times B,\quad\mathpzc D^{(2)}_{Z}(E,B):=ZB+\frac{1}{c}(0,E_{3},-E_{2})=ZB-\frac{1}{c}e_{1}\times E,

(2.57)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}Z=Z\partial_{t}+\partial_{x_{1}},\quad\partial_{x_{1}}Z=Z\partial_{x_{1}}+\frac{1}{c^{2}}\partial_{t},\quad Z\partial_{x_{2}}=\partial_{x_{2}}Z,\quad Z\partial_{x_{3}}=\partial_{x_{3}}Z,\quad\nabla Z=Z\nabla+e_{1}\frac{1}{c^{2}}\partial_{t}.

Using (
2.52
) we have that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c}\partial_{t}(ZE+\frac{1}{c}e_{1}\times B)\cdot e_{1}^{\prime}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c}(\partial_{t}ZE)\cdot e_{1}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{c^{2}}(e_{1}\times\partial_{t}B)\cdot e_{1}^{\prime}=\frac{1}{c}Z\partial_{t}E\cdot e_{1}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{c}(\partial_{x_{1}}E\cdot e_{1}^{\prime})+\frac{1}{c^{2}}(e_{1}\times\partial_{t}B)\cdot e_{1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(Z(\nabla\times B)\cdot e_{1}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{c}ZJ\cdot e_{1}^{\prime})+\frac{1}{c}(\partial_{x_{1}}E\cdot e_{1}^{\prime})+\frac{1}{c^{2}}(e_{1}\times\partial_{t}B)\cdot e_{1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle((\nabla Z\times B)\cdot e_{1}^{\prime}-\frac{1}{c^{2}}\partial_{t}(e_{1}\times B)\cdot e_{1}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{c}ZJ\cdot e_{1}^{\prime})+\frac{1}{c}(\partial_{x_{1}}E\cdot e_{1}^{\prime})+\frac{1}{c^{2}}(e_{1}\times\partial_{t}B)\cdot e_{1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(e_{1}^{\prime}\times\nabla)\cdot ZB+\frac{1}{c}ZJ\cdot e_{1}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{c}\partial_{x_{1}}E\cdot e_{1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r}\sum_{i,j}C_{i,j}(\theta,\phi)\Omega_{ij}E_{i}+\frac{1}{c}ZJ\cdot e_{1}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{c}\frac{x_{1}}{r}J_{0}+\frac{1}{r}\sum_{i\neq 1}\Omega_{i1}E_{i}.

Together with
\nabla\cdot(ZE)=Z\nabla\cdot E+\frac{1}{c^{2}}\partial_{t}(e_{1}\cdot E)=ZJ_{0}+\frac{1}{c^{2}}e_{1}\cdot J+\frac{1}{c}(e_{1}\cdot(\nabla\times B))
, (
2.6a
) and (
2.53
), we compute that

\displaystyle\partial_{r}(ZE+\frac{1}{c}e_{1}\times B)\cdot e_{1}^{\prime}=

\displaystyle(\partial_{r}(ZE_{i}))\cdot e_{1}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{c}\partial_{r}(e_{1}\times B)\cdot e_{1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot(ZE)+\frac{1}{r}\sum_{i,j}C_{i,j}(\theta,\phi)\Omega_{ij}(ZE_{i})+\frac{1}{c}\nabla\cdot(e_{1}\times B)+\frac{1}{r}\sum_{i,j,k}C_{i,j,k}(\theta,\phi)\Omega_{ij}B_{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle ZJ_{0}+\frac{1}{c^{2}}e_{1}\cdot J+\frac{1}{c}(e_{1}\cdot(\nabla\times B))+\frac{1}{r}\sum_{i,j}C_{i,j}(\theta,\phi)\Omega_{ij}(ZE_{i})+\frac{1}{c}\nabla\cdot(e_{1}\times B)+\frac{1}{r}\sum_{i,j,k}C_{i,j,k}(\theta,\phi)\Omega_{ij}B_{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle ZJ_{0}+\frac{1}{r}\sum_{i,j}C_{i,j}(\theta,\phi)\Omega_{ij}(ZE_{i})+\frac{1}{r}\sum_{i,j,k}C_{i,j,k}(\theta,\phi)\Omega_{ij}B_{k},

then we can finish the proof for
\rho
and
\sigma
by using Lemma
2.19
and (
2.50
).

Case 2:
\xi=\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
.

We just prove the
\alpha_{1}
term

\alpha_{1}(\mathpzc D_{Z}[E,B])=(ZB-\frac{1}{c}e_{1}\times E)\cdot e_{2}^{\prime}+(ZE+\frac{1}{c}e_{1}\times B)\cdot e_{3}^{\prime}=ZB\cdot e_{2}^{\prime}+ZE\cdot e_{3}^{\prime}-\frac{1}{c}(e_{1}\times E)\cdot e_{2}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{c}(e_{1}\times B)\cdot e_{3}^{\prime},

the
\alpha_{1}
term can be proved similarly. Remind (
2.6d
).
Using (
2.57
) we compute that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c}\partial_{t}\alpha_{1}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c}Z\partial_{t}B\cdot e_{2}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{c}\partial_{x_{1}}B\cdot e_{2}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{c}Z\partial_{t}E\cdot e_{3}^{\prime}+\partial_{x_{1}}E\cdot e_{3}^{\prime}-\frac{1}{c^{2}}(e_{1}\times\partial_{t}E)\cdot e_{2}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{c^{2}}(e_{1}\times\partial_{t}B)\cdot e_{2}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-Z(\nabla\times E)\cdot e_{2}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{c}\partial_{x_{1}}B\cdot e_{2}^{\prime}+Z(\nabla\times B)\cdot e_{3}^{\prime}+\partial_{x_{1}}E\cdot e_{3}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{c}J\cdot e_{3}^{\prime}-\frac{1}{c^{2}}(e_{1}\times\partial_{t}E)\cdot e_{2}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{c^{2}}(e_{1}\times\partial_{t}B)\cdot e_{2}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(\nabla\times ZE)\cdot e_{2}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{c^{2}}(e_{1}\times\partial_{t}E)\cdot e_{2}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{c}\partial_{x_{1}}B\cdot e_{2}^{\prime}+(\nabla\times ZB)\cdot e_{3}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{c}J\cdot e_{3}^{\prime}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{c^{2}}(e_{1}\times\partial_{t}B)\cdot e_{2}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{c}\partial_{x_{1}}E\cdot e_{3}^{\prime}-\frac{1}{c^{2}}(e_{1}\times\partial_{t}E)\cdot e_{2}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{c^{2}}(e_{1}\times\partial_{t}B)\cdot e_{2}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=
